<!-- thm-1: body_md -->
\label{explicitFformula}
We have
\begin{align}
&F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I} ) \nonumber \\
 =& \frac{\prod_{k=1}^{n} u_{k}^{-(N+n)}}{(n-r)!} \sum_{\sigma \in S_{n}} \sigma  \Biggl[ \prod_{j=1}^{r} \Bigg\{ (1 - q)(u_{j}^{2} - 1) \prod_{k=i_{j}+1}^{N} (u_{j} - q w_{k}) \prod_{k=1}^{i_{j}-1} (u_{j} - w_{k}) \Bigg\} \nonumber \\
&\quad \times \prod_{1 \le j < k \le r} \frac{q u_{j} - u_{k}}{u_{j} - u_{k}} (u_{j} u_{k} - 1) \prod_{j=r+1}^{n} \prod_{k=1}^{N} (u_{j} - q w_{k}) \nonumber \\
&\quad \times \prod_{j=r+1}^{n} \prod_{k=1}^{r} \frac{q u_{j} - u_{k}}{u_{j} - u_{k}} (u_{j} u_{k} - 1) \prod_{r+1 \le j < k \le n} (u_{j} u_{k} - q) \prod_{j=r+1}^{n} (\beta u_{j} - \gamma) \Biggr]. \label{Motherfunction}
\end{align}
Here, \(\sigma \in S_n\) acts by permuting the variables \(\mathbf{u}\), and \(S_n\) is the symmetric group.
<!-- end thm-1 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
We define \(WC_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})\)
and \(WB_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})\) by:
\begin{align}
&WC_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I} )
 :=  F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I} )|_{\gamma=0,\beta=1}
\label{WCfunction}
\end{align}

and
\begin{align}
&WB_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I} )
:= \left( \prod_{k=1}^{n}(1+u_k^{-1})^{-1} \right) F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I} )|_{\gamma=-1,\beta=1}
\label{WBfunction}
\end{align}
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\cite[Definition 2.1]{IkedaNaruse}
The factorial $K$-theoretic Schur $Q$- and $P$-functions are defined by
\begin{align}
GQ_\lambda(\mathbf{x}| \mathbf{b}) &:= \frac{1}{(n-r)!} \sum_{\sigma \in S_n} \sigma \left[ \prod_{i=1}^r (x_i \oplus x_i) \prod_{k=1}^{\lambda_i-1} (x_i \oplus b_k) \prod_{i=1}^r \prod_{j=i+1}^n \frac{x_i \oplus x_j}{x_i \ominus x_j} \right], \label{eq:GQexp} \\
GP_\lambda(\mathbf{x}| \mathbf{b}) &:= \frac{1}{(n-r)!} \sum_{\sigma \in S_n} \sigma \left[ \prod_{i=1}^r  \prod_{k=1}^{\lambda_i} (x_i \oplus b_k) \prod_{i=1}^r \prod_{j=i+1}^n \frac{x_i \oplus x_j}{x_i \ominus x_j} \right]. \label{eq:GPexp}
\end{align}
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\cite[(6.1), (6.2)]{IkedaNaruse}
We set
\begin{align}
GC_\lambda(\mathbf{x}|\mathbf{b}) &:=GQ_\lambda(\mathbf{x}|\mathbf{b}), \notag \\
GB_\lambda(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{b}) &:=GP_\lambda(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})|_{b_1=0,b_i \mapsto b_{i-1} (i \ge 2)}
\nonumber \\
&=
\frac{1}{(n-r)!} \sum_{\sigma \in S_n} \sigma \left[ \prod_{i=1}^r x_i  \prod_{k=1}^{\lambda_i-1} (x_i \oplus b_k) \prod_{i=1}^r \prod_{j=i+1}^n \frac{x_i \oplus x_j}{x_i \ominus x_j} \right]. \label{eq:GBexp}
\end{align}
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- prop-1: body_md -->
\label{reductionwbwc}
We have the following relations:
    \begin{align}
    &WC_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})|_{q=0}=GC_\lambda(\mathbf{x}|\mathbf{b})=GQ_\lambda(\mathbf{x}|\mathbf{b}), \\
      &WB_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})|_{q=0}=GB_\lambda(\mathbf{x}|\mathbf{b})
      =GP_\lambda(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})|_{b_1=0,b_i \to b_{i-1} (i \ge 2)}
      .
    \end{align}
    Here, the correspondence between the variables and labels is given by
    $x_i = 1 - u_i^{-1}$ ($i=1,\dots,n$), $b_k = 1 - w_k$ ($k=1,\dots,N$), and $i_j = \lambda_{r+1-j}$ ($j=1,\dots,r$).
<!-- end prop-1 -->
<!-- thm-2: body_md -->
\label{ffunctionproperties}
$\mathrm{(i)}$ We have
\begin{align}
F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})=0,
\end{align}
unless $\mathbf{M}\ge \mathbf{I}$, and
\begin{align}
&F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{I}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I} )
= (1 - q)^{\frac{(n-r)(n-r-1)}{2}} (\beta - \gamma)^{n-r} \nonumber \\
& \times \left( \prod_{j=1}^{r} w_{i_j}^{-(N+n)} \right) \prod_{j=1}^{r}
\left\{ (1 - q)(w_{i_j}^{2} - 1) \prod_{k=i_{j}+1}^{N} (w_{i_j} - q w_{k}) \prod_{k=1}^{i_{j}-1} (w_{i_j} - w_{k}) \right\} \nonumber \\
& \times \prod_{1 \le j < k \le r} \frac{q w_{i_j} - w_{i_k}}{w_{i_j} - w_{i_k}} (w_{i_j} w_{i_k} - 1)  \prod_{k=1}^N (1 - q w_k)^{n-r}  \prod_{k=1}^r (w_{i_k} - q) ^{n-r}.
\label{diagonalFfunction}
\end{align}

$\mathrm{(ii)}$
$(1-q)^{-r} F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$ is divisible by $(1-q)$ for every $\mathbf{M}\neq\mathbf{I}$, with no restriction that $|\mathbf{M}|=|\mathbf{I}|$.
<!-- end thm-2 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
We first prove $\mathrm{(i)}$.
Suppose first that $s\ge r$.
From the factor
$\displaystyle \sigma \Bigg( \prod_{j=1}^{r}(u_j^2-1) \Bigg)$
in the explicit formula \eqref{Motherfunction} for
$F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$,
the summation after the substitution
$\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}$ can be restricted to permutations
$\sigma\in S_n$ satisfying
$\{\sigma(1),\ldots,\sigma(r)\}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,s\}$.
Indeed, if $\sigma(j)>s$ for some $1\le j\le r$, then
$u_{\sigma(j)}=1$, and hence $u_{\sigma(j)}^2-1=0$.

For such a permutation, let
$K=(k_1,\ldots,k_r)$, where
$1\le k_1<\cdots<k_r\le N$, be determined by
$
\{u_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,u_{\sigma(r)}\}
=
\{w_{k_1},\ldots,w_{k_r}\}.
$
Then $K$ is an $r$-element subsequence of $\mathbf{M}$.
There exists a permutation $\tau\in S_r$ such that
$u_{\sigma(j)}=w_{k_{\tau(j)}}$ for $1\le j\le r$.
The factor
$\displaystyle
\sigma\left(
\prod_{j=1}^{r}\prod_{a=1}^{i_j-1}(u_j-w_a)
\right)
=
\prod_{j=1}^{r}\prod_{a=1}^{i_j-1}
(u_{\sigma(j)}-w_a)
$
shows that a nonzero summand must satisfy
$k_{\tau(j)}\ge i_j$ for $1\le j\le r$.

For each $j$, the $r-j+1$ distinct integers
$k_{\tau(j)},k_{\tau(j+1)},\ldots,k_{\tau(r)}$
are all greater than or equal to $i_j$, since
$i_j\le i_{j+1}\le\cdots\le i_r$.
Thus at most $j-1$ elements of $K$ are smaller than $i_j$,
and hence $k_j\ge i_j$.
Since $K$ is an $r$-element subsequence of
$\mathbf{M}=(m_1,\ldots,m_s)$, we have
$k_j\le m_{s-r+j}$ for $1\le j\le r$.
Consequently,
$
m_{s-r+j}\ge i_j
\qquad (1\le j\le r),
$
and hence $\mathbf{M}\ge\mathbf{I}$. Therefore,
$F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})=0$
unless $\mathbf{M}\ge\mathbf{I}$.

We next evaluate the diagonal specialization $\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{I}$.
In this case $s=r$. A surviving summand must satisfy
$u_{\sigma(j)}=w_{i_{\tau(j)}}$ and
$i_{\tau(j)}\ge i_j$ for $1\le j\le r$, for some
$\tau\in S_r$.
Since $i_1<\cdots<i_r$, these inequalities force
$\tau=\mathrm{id}$. It follows that
$\sigma(j)=j$ for $1\le j\le r$, while
$
\{\sigma(k):r+1\le k\le n\}
=
\{r+1,\ldots,n\}.
$
Since $u_{r+1}=\cdots=u_n=1$, the $(n-r)!$ permutations
of these variables give the same contribution.
Thus the factor $(n-r)!$ produced by the sum cancels the factor
$1/(n-r)!$ in \eqref{Motherfunction}.
To make the remaining evaluation explicit, for each $b$ with
$r+1\le b\le n$ we have
$
\displaystyle
\left.\prod_{k=1}^N(u_b-qw_k)\right|_{u_b=1}
=
\prod_{k=1}^N(1-qw_k).
$
For $1\le a\le r$ and $r+1\le b\le n$,
$\displaystyle
\left.
\frac{qu_b-u_a}{u_b-u_a}(u_bu_a-1)
\right|_{u_b=1,\,u_a=w_{i_a}}
=
\frac{q-w_{i_a}}{1-w_{i_a}}(w_{i_a}-1)
=w_{i_a}-q.
$
For every pair $r+1\le a<b\le n$, the factor $u_au_b-q$
becomes $1-q$, and each factor $\beta u_b-\gamma$ becomes $\beta-\gamma$.
Therefore the factors involving $u_{r+1},\ldots,u_n$ give
$\displaystyle
(1-q)^{\frac{(n-r)(n-r-1)}{2}}(\beta-\gamma)^{n-r}
\prod_{k=1}^N(1-qw_k)^{n-r}
\prod_{a=1}^r(w_{i_a}-q)^{n-r}.
$
Multiplying this by the remaining factors in the identity summand,
with $u_a=w_{i_a}$ for $1\le a\le r$, gives exactly
\eqref{diagonalFfunction}. Thus no additional factor is hidden in the
cancellation of the $(n-r)!$ identical summands with the prefactor
$1/(n-r)!$.

Finally, suppose that $s<r$.
Repeated application of Lemma~\ref{onevariablesp}
which will be shown later
gives
$
F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
=
C_{N,n,r,\mathbf{M}}
F_{N,r,r}
(
w_{m_1},\ldots,w_{m_s},
\underbrace{1,\ldots,1}_{r-s}
|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}
)
$
where $C_{N,n,r,\mathbf{M}}$ is the product of the factors obtained
from Lemma~\ref{onevariablesp}.

For $n=r$, the factor
$\displaystyle \sigma\Biggl(\prod_{j=1}^{r}(u_j^2-1)\Biggr)
=\allowbreak\prod_{j=1}^{r}(u_j^2-1)$
appears in every summand.
Since $s<r$, the factorization in Lemma~\ref{factorizationlemma}
shows that the specialization vanishes:
$F_{N,r,r}\bigl(w_{m_1},\ldots,w_{m_s},\allowbreak
1,\ldots,1|\allowbreak\mathbf w|\mathbf I\bigr)=0$.
It follows that
$F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})=0$.
Since $\mathbf{M}\not\ge\mathbf{I}$ when $s<r$, this completes the proof of
$\mathrm{(i)}$. \\
\\
 We next prove $\mathrm{(ii)}$.
The factor $(1-q)^r$ is explicit in \eqref{Motherfunction}, so
$(1-q)^{-r}F_{N,n,r}$ is regular as a polynomial in $q$.
It is therefore enough to set $q=1$ before making the
specialization $\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}$. At $q=1$, all quotients
$(qu_j-u_k)/(u_j-u_k)$ become $1$, and every summand contains the factor
$\displaystyle
\prod_{j=1}^{r}
\Bigg\{
(u_{\sigma(j)}^2-1)
\prod_{\substack{k=1\\k\ne i_j}}^N
(u_{\sigma(j)}-w_k)
\Bigg\}.
$
After the specialization, this factor can be nonzero only if
$u_{\sigma(j)}=w_{i_j}$ for every $j=1,\ldots,r$.
Hence every element of $\mathbf{I}$ must occur in $\mathbf{M}$.
If $s<r$, or if $\mathbf{I}\not\subseteq\mathbf{M}$, every summand is zero.
Suppose next that $\mathbf{I}\subseteq\mathbf{M}$ and $s>r$.
Then one of the values $w_m$ with
$m\in\mathbf{M}\setminus\mathbf{I}$ must occur among
$u_{\sigma(r+1)},\ldots,u_{\sigma(n)}$.
For that variable, say $u_{\sigma(b)}=w_m$ with $r+1\le b\le n$, the factor
$\displaystyle
\prod_{k=1}^N(u_{\sigma(b)}-w_k)
$
contains $w_m-w_m=0$.
Thus the value at $q=1$ is zero for every
$\mathbf{M}\ne\mathbf{I}$, independently of the cardinality of
$\mathbf{M}$. This proves divisibility by $(1-q)$.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
We define type $C$ $\mathbf{M}$-smallness only in the equal-cardinality case $s=r$.  Thus, for an $r$-element subset $\mathbf{M}=\{m_{1}<\cdots<m_{r}\}\subset\{1,\ldots,N\}$, we define the Laurent polynomial $f$ to be type $C$ \emph{$\mathbf{M}$-small} if
\begin{align}
\varphi_{\mathbf{M}}(\mathcal{N}(f)) \subset [-r(r+1), (N-r)(2N-r+2)-1].
\end{align}
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
We introduce the normalization factor $\mathcal{E}^{C}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})$ and define the normalized weight function as follows:
\begin{equation}
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) := WC_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) \cdot \mathcal{E}^C_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})^{-1}.
\label{typeCnormalizeddefinition}
\end{equation}
Here, the normalization factor $\mathcal{E}_{N,n,r}^C(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})$, which is symmetric with respect to $\mathbf{u}$ and independent of $\mathbf{I}$, is defined as follows:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{E}_{N,n,r}^C(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w}) =  \frac{1}{(1-q)^{n-r}} \frac{ \prod_{j,k=1}^n (1 - q u_j^{-1} u_k) \prod_{j=1}^n \prod_{k=1}^N (1 - q u_j w_k) }{ \prod_{1 \le j < k \le n} (1 - q u_j u_k) \prod_{1 \le j \le k \le N} (1 - q w_j w_k) }.
\label{typeCnormalizationfactor}
\end{equation}
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- thm-3: body_md -->
\label{typeCmainproperties}
Let $|\mathbf{M}|=|\mathbf{I}|=r$ and $\mathbf{M}=\{m_1<\cdots<m_r\}\subset\{1,\dots,N\}$. Then
$\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}
(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$ satisfies the following properties. 

$\mathrm{(i)}$
We have
\begin{align}
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})=0,
\end{align}
unless $\mathbf{M} \ge \mathbf{I}$, and
\begin{equation}
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{I}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) = \prod_{\alpha \in \operatorname{Inv}(\lambda)} (1 - e^{-\alpha}) \prod_{\alpha \in T(\lambda)} (1 - q e^{+\alpha}). \label{normalizationWC}
\end{equation}

$\mathrm{(ii)}$
$\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}
(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$
is divisible by
\begin{align}
&\prod_{1\le j\le k\le r}
(1-w_{m_j}^{-1}w_{m_k}^{-1})
\prod_{\substack{j \le k \\ j, k \in \{1, 2, \dots, N\} \setminus \mathbf{M}}} (1 - q w_j w_{k}) 
\prod_{j \in \{1, 2, \dots, N\} \setminus \mathbf{M}} \prod_{l: m_l < j} (1-q w_{m_l}^{-1} w_j).
\label{typeCfullDivisibility}
\end{align}

$\mathrm{(iii)}$
$\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}
(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$ is type $C$ $\mathbf{M}$-small if $\mathbf{M} \neq \mathbf{I}$.
<!-- end thm-3 -->
<!-- prop-2: body_md -->
Fix $\mathbf{I}$, and suppose that a collection of Laurent polynomials
$(F_{\mathbf{M}})_{|\mathbf{M}|=r}$ satisfies the following conditions.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] If $\mathbf{M}\not\ge \mathbf{I}$, then $F_{\mathbf{M}}=0$, and
\begin{align*}
F_{\mathbf{I}}
=&
\prod_{1\le j\le k\le r}
(1-w_{i_j}^{-1}w_{i_k}^{-1})
\prod_{j\in \mathbf{I}^{\mathrm c}}
\prod_{l:i_l>j}
(1-w_{i_l}^{-1}w_j) \notag \\
&\times\prod_{\substack{j\le k\\j,k\in \mathbf{I}^{\mathrm c}}}
(1-qw_jw_k)
\prod_{j\in \mathbf{I}^{\mathrm c}}
\prod_{l:i_l<j}
(1-qw_{i_l}^{-1}w_j).
\end{align*}

\item[(ii)] The Laurent polynomial $F_{\mathbf{M}}$ is divisible by
\[
\prod_{1\le j\le k\le r}
(1-w_{m_j}^{-1}w_{m_k}^{-1})
\prod_{\substack{j\le k\\j,k\in \mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}}
(1-qw_jw_k)
\prod_{j\in \mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}
\prod_{l:m_l<j}
(1-qw_{m_l}^{-1}w_j).
\]

\item[(iii)] For $k\in \mathbf{M}$ and $j\notin \mathbf{M}$,
\[
F_{\mathbf{M}}-F_{(\mathbf{M}\setminus\{k\})\cup\{j\}}
\in(1-w_j/w_k).
\]

\item[(iv)] If $\mathbf{M}\ne \mathbf{I}$, then
\[
\varphi_{\mathbf{M}}\bigl(\mathcal N(F_{\mathbf{M}})\bigr)
\subset
[-r(r+1),(N-r)(2N-r+2)-1].
\]
\end{enumerate}
Then there is at most one such collection
$(F_{\mathbf{M}})_{|\mathbf{M}|=r}$.
<!-- end prop-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
The proof is essentially the same as in \cite{RTV}.
Let $(F_{\mathbf{M}})_{|\mathbf{M}|=r}$ and
$(G_{\mathbf{M}})_{|\mathbf{M}|=r}$ be two collections satisfying
conditions~(i)--(iv), and set
$
\Delta_{\mathbf{M}}=F_{\mathbf{M}}-G_{\mathbf{M}}.
$
Choose a minimal $\mathbf{M}$ in the componentwise order among the indices for
which $\Delta_{\mathbf{M}}\ne0$.
The diagonal values agree, so $\mathbf{M}\ne \mathbf{I}$.
If $j\notin \mathbf{M}$, $k\in \mathbf{M}$, and $j<k$, set
$
\mathbf{M}'=(\mathbf{M}\setminus\{k\})\cup\{j\}.
$
Then $\mathbf{M}'<\mathbf{M}$, and hence
$\Delta_{\mathbf{M}'}=0$ by the minimality of $\mathbf{M}$.
Applying condition~(iii) to the two collections and subtracting the resulting
congruences gives
$\displaystyle
\Delta_{\mathbf{M}}-\Delta_{\mathbf{M}'}
\in(1-w_j/w_k).
$
Therefore $\Delta_{\mathbf{M}}$ is divisible by $1-w_k^{-1}w_j$.
Multiplying over all such pairs shows that $\Delta_{\mathbf{M}}$ is divisible by
$\displaystyle
\prod_{j\in \mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}
\prod_{l:m_l>j}
(1-w_{m_l}^{-1}w_j).
$
The coefficient ring
$\mathbb{C}(q)[w_1^{\pm1},\ldots,w_N^{\pm1}]$ is a unique factorization domain,
and the factors $1-w_{m_l}^{-1}w_j$ obtained here are relatively prime to the
factors in condition~(ii). Therefore $\Delta_{\mathbf{M}}$ is divisible by
\begin{align*}
&\prod_{1\le j\le k\le r}
(1-w_{m_j}^{-1}w_{m_k}^{-1})
\prod_{j\in \mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}
\prod_{l:m_l>j}
(1-w_{m_l}^{-1}w_j)
\prod_{\substack{j\le k\\j,k\in \mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}}
(1-qw_jw_k)
\prod_{j\in \mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}
\prod_{l:m_l<j}
(1-qw_{m_l}^{-1}w_j).
\end{align*}

The degree interval of the first product is
$
[-r(r+1),0].
$
The degree interval of the second product is
$
\left[
0,\,
\left|\{(j,l): j\in\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c},\ m_l>j\}\right|
\right].
$
The degree interval of the third product is
$
[0,2(N-r)(N-r+1)].
$
The degree interval of the fourth product is
$
\left[
0,\,
\left|\{(j,l): j\in\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c},\ m_l<j\}\right|
\right].
$
The sum of the upper endpoints of the second and fourth intervals is $r(N-r)$.
Consequently, the product of all four factors has degree interval
$
[-r(r+1),(N-r)(2N-r+2)].
$
Thus $\Delta_{\mathbf{M}}$ is the displayed product times a Laurent polynomial $g$.
Suppose that $g\ne0$ and write
$
\varphi_{\mathbf{M}}\bigl(\mathcal N(g)\bigr)=[a,b].
$
Then $a\le b$. The additivity of the lower and upper Newton degrees under
multiplication gives
$
\varphi_{\mathbf{M}}\bigl(\mathcal N(\Delta_{\mathbf{M}})\bigr)
=
[-r(r+1)+a,(N-r)(2N-r+2)+b].
$
Since $\mathbf{M}\ne\mathbf{I}$, condition~(iv) applies to both candidates and
therefore also to their difference. Hence
$
\varphi_{\mathbf{M}}\bigl(\mathcal N(\Delta_{\mathbf{M}})\bigr)
\subset
[-r(r+1),(N-r)(2N-r+2)-1].
$
Comparing the two endpoints yields $a\ge0$ and $b\le-1$, contradicting
$a\le b$. Thus $g=0$, and hence $\Delta_{\mathbf{M}}=0$, contrary to the
choice of $\mathbf{M}$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- prop-3: body_md -->
Fix $\mathbf{I}$ and set
\[
F_{\mathbf{M}}
=
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}
(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
\qquad (|\mathbf{M}|=r).
\]
This collection satisfies all conditions (i)--(iv) in the preceding uniqueness proposition.
Consequently, these specialization values are uniquely determined by these conditions.
<!-- end prop-3 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Conditions (i), (ii), and (iv) follow from Theorem~\ref{typeCmainproperties}.

Condition (iii) follows directly from symmetry in the $\mathbf{u}$-variables. Let $k\in\mathbf{M}$ and $j\notin\mathbf{M}$, and set
$
\mathbf{M}'=(\mathbf{M}\setminus\{k\})\cup\{j\}.
$
When $w_j=w_k$, the sequences $\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}$ and
$\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}'}$ agree up to permutation. Therefore
$F_{\mathbf{M}}-F_{\mathbf{M}'}$ vanishes when $w_j=w_k$. Since both terms are Laurent polynomials,
$
F_{\mathbf{M}}-F_{\mathbf{M}'}
\in (1-w_j/w_k).
$
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
We define type $B$ $\mathbf{M}$-smallness only in the equal-cardinality case $s=r$.  Thus, for an $r$-element subset $\mathbf{M}=\{m_{1}<\cdots<m_{r}\}\subset\{1,\ldots,N\}$, we define the Laurent polynomial $f$ to be type $B$ \emph{$\mathbf{M}$-small} if
\begin{align}
\varphi_{\mathbf{M}}(\mathcal{N}(f)) \subset [-r^2, (N-r)(2N-r)-1].
\end{align}
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
We introduce the normalization factor $\mathcal{E}^B_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})$ and define the normalized weight function as follows:
\begin{equation}
\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) := WB_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) \cdot \mathcal{E}^B_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})^{-1}.
\label{typeBnormalizeddefinition}
\end{equation}
Here, the normalization factor $\mathcal{E}^B_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})$, which is symmetric with respect to the variables $\mathbf{u}$ and independent of $\mathbf{I}$, is defined as follows:
\begin{align}
&\mathcal{E}^B_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w}) 
:= (1-q)^{-(n-r)}
\nonumber \\
&\times\frac{ \prod_{j,k=1}^n (1 - q u_j^{-1} u_k) \prod_{j=1}^n \prod_{k=1}^N (1 - q u_j w_k) \prod_{j=1}^n (1 - q u_j) }{ \prod_{1 \le j < k \le n} (1 - q u_j u_k) \prod_{j=1}^n (1 - q u_j^2) \prod_{1 \le j < k \le N} (1 - q w_j w_k) \prod_{k=1}^N (1 - q w_k) }.
\label{typeBnormalizationfactor}
\end{align}
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- thm-4: body_md -->
\label{typeBmainproperties}
Let $|\mathbf{M}|=|\mathbf{I}|=r$ and $\mathbf{M}=\{m_1<\cdots<m_r\}\subset\{1,\dots,N\}$. Then
$\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}
(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$ satisfies the following properties. 

$\mathrm{(i)}$
We have
\begin{align}
\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})=0,
\end{align}
unless $\mathbf{M} \ge \mathbf{I}$, and
\begin{equation}
\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{I}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) = \prod_{\alpha \in \operatorname{Inv}(\lambda)} (1 - e^{-\alpha}) \prod_{\alpha \in T(\lambda)} (1 - q e^{+\alpha}).
\label{normalizationWB}
\end{equation}

$\mathrm{(ii)}$
$\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}
(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$
is divisible by
\begin{align}
&\prod_{j=1}^{r}(1-w_{m_j}^{-1})
\prod_{1\le j<k\le r}(1-w_{m_j}^{-1}w_{m_k}^{-1})
\prod_{j \in \{1, 2, \dots, N\} \setminus \mathbf{M}} (1 - q w_j) \nonumber\\
&\quad\times
\prod_{\substack{j < k \\ j, k \in \{1, 2, \dots, N\} \setminus \mathbf{M}}} (1 - q w_j w_{k})
 \prod_{j \in \{1, 2, \dots, N\} \setminus \mathbf{M}} \prod_{l: m_l < j} (1-q w_{m_l}^{-1} w_j).
\label{typeBfullDivisibility}
\end{align}


$\mathrm{(iii)}$
$\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}
(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$ is type $B$ $\mathbf{M}$-small if $\mathbf{M} \neq \mathbf{I}$.
<!-- end thm-4 -->
<!-- prop-4: body_md -->
Fix $\mathbf{I}$, and suppose that a collection of Laurent polynomials
$(F_{\mathbf{M}})_{|\mathbf{M}|=r}$ satisfies the following conditions.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] If $\mathbf{M}\not\ge\mathbf{I}$, then $F_{\mathbf{M}}=0$, and
\begin{align*}
F_{\mathbf{I}}
={}&
\prod_{j=1}^{r}(1-w_{i_j}^{-1})
\prod_{1\le j<k\le r}(1-w_{i_j}^{-1}w_{i_k}^{-1})
\prod_{j\in\mathbf{I}^{\mathrm c}}
\prod_{l:i_l>j}(1-w_{i_l}^{-1}w_j)
\notag\\
&\times
\prod_{j\in\mathbf{I}^{\mathrm c}}(1-qw_j)
\prod_{\substack{j<k\\j,k\in\mathbf{I}^{\mathrm c}}}(1-qw_jw_k)
\prod_{j\in\mathbf{I}^{\mathrm c}}
\prod_{l:i_l<j}(1-qw_{i_l}^{-1}w_j).
\end{align*}

\item[(ii)] The Laurent polynomial $F_{\mathbf{M}}$ is divisible by
\begin{align*}
&\prod_{j=1}^{r}(1-w_{m_j}^{-1})
\prod_{1\le j<k\le r}(1-w_{m_j}^{-1}w_{m_k}^{-1})
\prod_{j\in\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}(1-qw_j)
\notag\\
&\qquad\times
\prod_{\substack{j<k\\j,k\in\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}}(1-qw_jw_k)
\prod_{j\in\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}
\prod_{l:m_l<j}(1-qw_{m_l}^{-1}w_j).
\end{align*}

\item[(iii)] For $k\in\mathbf{M}$ and $j\notin\mathbf{M}$,
\[
F_{\mathbf{M}}-F_{(\mathbf{M}\setminus\{k\})\cup\{j\}}
\in(1-w_j/w_k).
\]

\item[(iv)] If $\mathbf{M}\ne\mathbf{I}$, then
\[
\varphi_{\mathbf{M}}\bigl(\mathcal N(F_{\mathbf{M}})\bigr)
\subset[-r^2,(N-r)(2N-r)-1].
\]
\end{enumerate}
Then there is at most one such collection
$(F_{\mathbf{M}})_{|\mathbf{M}|=r}$.
<!-- end prop-4 -->
<!-- prop-5: body_md -->
Fix $\mathbf{I}$ and set
\[
F_{\mathbf{M}}
=
\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
\qquad(|\mathbf{M}|=r).
\]
This collection satisfies all conditions (i)--(iv) in the preceding uniqueness proposition.
Consequently, these specialization values are uniquely determined by these conditions.
<!-- end prop-5 -->
<!-- prop-6: body_md -->
\label{diddiffbeforenorm}  Let $i \in \{1, \dots, N-1\}$. \\
   (i)  If $i+1 = i_k \in \mathbf{I}$ and $i \notin \mathbf{I}$, we have
    \begin{align}
    &F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}^{(i)}) \nonumber \\
    =&\frac{1}{w_i-w_{i+1}}
    \Big(
    (w_i-q w_{i+1}) F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|s_i \mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
    -(1-q)w_{i+1} F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
    \Big), \label{recrelone} \\
    &F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) \nonumber \\
    =&\frac{1}{q(w_i-w_{i+1})}
    \Big(
    (w_i-q w_{i+1}) F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|s_i \mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}^{(i)})
    -(1-q)w_{i} F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}^{(i)})
    \Big), \label{recreltwo}
    \end{align}
    where 
$\mathbf{I}^{(i)} = \{ i_1, \dots, i_{k-1}, i, i_{k+1}, \dots, i_r \}.
$ \\
\\
(ii) If $i,i+1 \in \mathbf{I}$ or $i,i+1 \not\in \mathbf{I}$
, we have
\begin{align}
F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
=F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|s_i \mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}). \label{recrelthree}
\end{align}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{recone.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{rectwo.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{recthree.pdf}
  \caption{Recursive relation \eqref{recrelone}.}
  \label{fig:basicrecursion}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{recfour.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{recfive.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{recsix.pdf}
  \caption{Recursive relation \eqref{recreltwo}.}
  \label{fig:basicrecursiontwo}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{recseven.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{receight.pdf}
  \caption{Commutativity \eqref{recrelthree}.}
  \label{fig:basicrecursionthree}
\end{figure}
<!-- end prop-6 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
The derivation of relations of this type by the railroad argument
using the Yang--Baxter equation \eqref{eq:YBE} is well known
(cf.~\cite{GGMW}).
Rearranging the resulting relations gives
\eqref{recrelone}--\eqref{recrelthree}, whose graphical forms
are shown in Figures~\ref{fig:basicrecursion},
\ref{fig:basicrecursiontwo}, and
\ref{fig:basicrecursionthree}, respectively.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- prop-7: body_md -->
(i) If $1 \in \mathbf{I}$, we have
\begin{align}
    &F_{N,n,r-1}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}^{(0)}) \nonumber \\
    =&\frac{1}{1-w_1^2}
    \Bigg(
    (1-q)^{-1} \prod_{j=2}^{N} (1-q w_1w_j)^{-1} \prod_{j=1}^n (1-q w_1^{-1} u_j^{-1})^{-1}  \nonumber \\
    &\times
    F_{N,n+1,r}(\mathbf{u},w_1^{-1}|w_1^{-1},w_2,\dotsm,w_N|\mathbf{I})
    - (\beta w_1^2-\gamma w_1) F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) \Bigg),
    \label{pizerorecursionone}
\end{align}
where $\mathbf{I}^{(0)}=\{ i_2,\dotsm,i_r \}$.
\\
(ii) If $1 \not\in \mathbf{I}$, we have
\begin{align}
 &F_{N,n+1,r}(\mathbf{u},w_1^{-1}|w_1^{-1},w_2,\dotsm,w_N|\mathbf{I})
 \nonumber \\
 =& (1-q) (\beta-\gamma w_1) \prod_{j=2}^{N} (1-q w_1w_j) \prod_{j=1}^n (1-q w_1^{-1} u_j^{-1})
 F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}).
 \label{pizerorecursiontwo}
\end{align}



\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{pizerorecone.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{pizerorectwo.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{pizerorecthree.pdf}
  \caption{Recursive relation \eqref{pizerorecursionone}.}
  \label{fig:pizerorecursionone}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{pizerorecfour.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{pizerorecfive.pdf}
  \caption{Reduction \eqref{pizerorecursiontwo}.}
  \label{fig:pizerorecursiontwo}
\end{figure}
<!-- end prop-7 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Graphical descriptions of
\eqref{pizerorecursionone} and \eqref{pizerorecursiontwo}
are given in Figures~\ref{fig:pizerorecursionone} and \ref{fig:pizerorecursiontwo}, respectively.
The two identities are proved in the same way, so we prove \eqref{pizerorecursionone}.


The graphical deformation process is depicted in Figure~\ref{fig:pizerorecursionthree}. 
We begin with \\
$F_{N,n+1,r}(\mathbf{u}, w_1^{-1}|w_1^{-1}, w_2, \dots, w_N|\mathbf{I})$. 
Here, we observe an $R$-matrix that essentially reduces to the permutation operator \eqref{eq:Ruu} $R(w_1^{-1}, w_1^{-1}) = (1-q)P$. 
By applying the Yang--Baxter equation \eqref{eq:YBE} repeatedly, we move the line to which the spectral parameter $w_1^{-1}$ is assigned toward the upper-right region. 
Due to the ice rule \eqref{eq:ice-rule}, the configuration involving this line is uniquely determined; thus, we can remove the line and instead multiply by the overall factor 
$\displaystyle
\prod_{j=2}^{N} (1-q w_1 w_j) \prod_{j=1}^n (1-q w_1^{-1} u_j^{-1}),
$
which consists of the $R$-matrix elements \eqref{eq:R-components}. Finally, we decompose the partition function into two terms based on the $K$-matrix elements \eqref{eq:Ktri-components}, leading to the final equation
\begin{align}
&(1-q)
\prod_{j=2}^{N} (1-q w_1 w_j) \prod_{j=1}^n (1-q w_1^{-1} u_j^{-1})
\nonumber \\
&\times
 \Big(
 (\beta w_1^2-\gamma w_1) F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) 
 +
 (1-w_1^2)
 F_{N,n,r-1}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}^{(0)}) 
     \Big). \notag
\end{align}
The graphical identity in Figure~\ref{fig:pizerorecursionthree} is equivalent to Figure~\ref{fig:pizerorecursionone}, and algebraically gives \eqref{pizerorecursionone}.





\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{pizerorecsix.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{pizerorecseven.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{pizeroreceight.pdf}
 \includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{pizerorecnine.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{pizerorecten.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{pizeroreceleven.pdf}
 \includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{pizerorectwelve.pdf}
  \caption{The graphical deformation process to get \eqref{pizerorecursionone}.}
  \label{fig:pizerorecursionthree}
\end{figure}
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
(i) If $1 \in \mathbf{I}$, we have
\begin{align}
    &WC_{N,n,r-1}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}^{(0)}) \nonumber \\
    =&\frac{1}{1-w_1^2}
    \Bigg(
    (1-q)^{-1} \prod_{j=2}^{N} (1-q w_1w_j)^{-1} \prod_{j=1}^n (1-q w_1^{-1} u_j^{-1})^{-1}  \nonumber \\
    &\times
WC_{N,n+1,r}(\mathbf{u},w_1^{-1}|w_1^{-1},w_2,\dotsm,w_N|\mathbf{I})
    - w_1^2 WC_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) \Bigg),
    \label{pizerorecursiononeWC} \\
    &WB_{N,n,r-1}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}^{(0)}) \nonumber \\
    =&\frac{1}{1-w_1}
    \Bigg(
    (1-q)^{-1} \prod_{j=2}^{N} (1-q w_1w_j)^{-1} \prod_{j=1}^n (1-q w_1^{-1} u_j^{-1})^{-1}  \nonumber \\
    &\times
WB_{N,n+1,r}(\mathbf{u},w_1^{-1}|w_1^{-1},w_2,\dotsm,w_N|\mathbf{I})
    - w_1 WB_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) \Bigg),
    \label{pizerorecursiononeWB}
\end{align}
where $\mathbf{I}^{(0)}=\{ i_2,\dotsm,i_r \}$.
\\
(ii) If $1 \not\in \mathbf{I}$, we have
\begin{align}
 &WC_{N,n+1,r}(\mathbf{u},w_1^{-1}|w_1^{-1},w_2,\dotsm,w_N|\mathbf{I})
 \nonumber \\
 =& (1-q)  \prod_{j=2}^{N} (1-q w_1w_j) \prod_{j=1}^n (1-q w_1^{-1} u_j^{-1})
 WC_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}),
 \label{pizerorecursiontwoWC} \\
 &WB_{N,n+1,r}(\mathbf{u},w_1^{-1}|w_1^{-1},w_2,\dotsm,w_N|\mathbf{I})
 \nonumber \\
 =& (1-q)  \prod_{j=2}^{N} (1-q w_1w_j) \prod_{j=1}^n (1-q w_1^{-1} u_j^{-1})
 WB_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}).
 \label{pizerorecursiontwoWB}
\end{align}
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- lem-1: body_md -->
\label{deltalem} We have
\begin{align}
    F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})|_{u_1=\cdots=u_n=1}
    =(\beta-\gamma)^n (1-q)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}} \prod_{j=1}^N (1-q w_j)^n \delta_{\mathbf{I},\emptyset}. \label{specializetoone}
    \end{align}
    Here $\delta_{\mathbf{I},\emptyset}$ means 1 if $\mathbf{I}$ is an empty set $(r=0)$, and 0 otherwise.



\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{deltaone.pdf}
  \caption{Specializing all $u$-variables to 1 \eqref{specializetoone}.}
  \label{fig:spone}
\end{figure}
<!-- end lem-1 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Consider a diagram where the configuration of $0$'s and $1$'s at the bottom boundary is not fixed
(Figure~\ref{fig:delta}, top left panel).
Since the $K$-matrix \eqref{eq:Ktri-components} reduces essentially to the identity matrix $K(1)=(\beta-\gamma) \mathrm{Id}$ and the $R$-matrix satisfies the ice rule \eqref{eq:ice-rule}, the configuration is uniquely determined row by row starting from the top, as illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:delta}. This implies that the partition function is nonzero if the bottom row consists entirely of $0$'s (i.e., $\mathbf{I}=\emptyset$), and vanishes otherwise.
If $\mathbf{I} = \emptyset$, a direct calculation shows that
the product of the elements of the $K$-matrices and $R$-matrices
for the unique configuration is
$(\beta-\gamma)^n (1-q)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}} \prod_{j=1}^N (1-q w_j)^n$,
leading to \eqref{specializetoone}
(Figure~\ref{fig:spone}).


\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
 \includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{deltatwo.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{deltathree.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{deltafour.pdf}
 \includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{deltafive.pdf}
  \caption{The freezing process starting from the top row.}
  \label{fig:delta}
\end{figure}
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
We have
    \begin{align}
    &WC_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})|_{u_1=\cdots=u_n=1}
    =
     WB_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})|_{u_1=\cdots=u_n=1}
     \nonumber \\
    =& (1-q)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}} \prod_{j=1}^N (1-q w_j)^n \delta_{\mathbf{I},\emptyset}. \label{whenalltoone}
    \end{align}
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- lem-2: body_md -->
\label{onevariablesp}
Assume $r\le n-1$. We have
\begin{align}
&F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})|_{u_n=1}
=(\beta-\gamma) \prod_{k=1}^{n-1} (1-qu_k^{-1}) \prod_{j=1}^{N} (1-q w_j)
F_{N,n-1,r}(u_1,\dots,u_{n-1}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}).
\label{Fonevariablesp}
\end{align}
<!-- end lem-2 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
The proof proceeds in the same way as that of Lemma~\ref{deltalem}, using the ice rule \eqref{eq:ice-rule}. Consider the first panel of Figure~\ref{fig:forstability}. When $u_n=1$, the configuration of the first row is uniquely determined, as shown in the second panel. Hence we may remove the first row at the cost of multiplying by
$\displaystyle (\beta-\gamma)\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}(1-qu_k^{-1}) \prod_{j=1}^{N}(1-qw_j)$. After removing the first row, the remaining partition function is
$F_{N,n-1,r}(u_1,\dots,u_{n-1}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$. Therefore, we obtain \eqref{Fonevariablesp}.


\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{forstabilityone.pdf}
 \includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{forstabilitytwo.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{forstabilitythree.pdf}
\caption{A graphical derivation of \eqref{Fonevariablesp}.}
  \label{fig:forstability}
\end{figure}
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
We introduce type $A$ trigonometric weight functions:
\begin{align}
    W_{N,n}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
    :=&\prod_{k=1}^n u_k^{-N}
    \sum_{\sigma \in S_n} \sigma
    \Bigg[
    \prod_{j=1}^n \Bigg\{ (1-q)u_j \prod_{k=i_j+1}^N (u_j-qw_k) \prod_{k=1}^{i_j-1} (u_j-w_k) \Bigg\} \nonumber \\
    &\times
    \prod_{1 \le j < k \le n} \frac{qu_j-u_k}{u_j-u_k}
\Bigg].
\end{align}
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- lem-3: body_md -->
\label{factorizationlemma}
    If $r=n$, we have
    \begin{align}
        F_{N,n,n}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
        =\prod_{j=1}^n (1-u_j^{-2}) \prod_{1 \le i < j \le n} (1-u_i^{-1} u_j^{-1})  W_{N,n}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}).
        \label{factorizationlemmaidentity}
    \end{align}
<!-- end lem-3 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Starting from the top-left panel of Figure~\ref{fig:factorization}, we observe that for $r=n$, the ice rule \eqref{eq:ice-rule} restricts all states on the edges between the triangular and rectangular parts to be $1$. This restriction further fixes the boundary $K$-matrix elements to $[K(u_j)]_{0}^1$ in \eqref{eq:Ktri-components}, uniquely determining the configuration of the triangular part (see the middle-right panel). Removing this triangular part introduces the factor 
$\displaystyle
    \prod_{j=1}^n (1-u_j^{-2}) \prod_{1 \le i < j \le n} (1-u_i^{-1} u_j^{-1}).
$
The remaining rectangular part is the partition function $W_{N,n}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$. Hence we arrive at the factorization identity \eqref{factorizationlemmaidentity}.


\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
   \includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{factorone.pdf}
 \includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{factortwo.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{factorthree.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{factorfour.pdf}
 \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{factorfive.pdf}
  \caption{The factorization \eqref{factorizationlemmaidentity} when $r=n$.}
  \label{fig:factorization}
\end{figure}
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
If $r=n$, we have
    \begin{align}
        WC_{N,n,n}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
        &=\prod_{j=1}^n (1-u_j^{-2}) \prod_{1 \le i < j \le n} (1-u_i^{-1} u_j^{-1})  W_{N,n}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}), \label{typeCnrFactorization}\\
           WB_{N,n,n}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
        &=\prod_{j=1}^n (1-u_j^{-1}) \prod_{1 \le i < j \le n} (1-u_i^{-1} u_j^{-1})  W_{N,n}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}). \label{typeBnrFactorization}
    \end{align}
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- prop-8: body_md -->
Assume $r\le n-2$. If $u_i u_j=1$, we have
\begin{align}
     F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})|_{u_i u_j=1} 
     &=(1-q)(\beta-\gamma u_i )  (\beta-\gamma u_{j}) 
     \prod_{k=1}^N \{ (1-qu_i w_k)(1-qu_j w_k)  \} \nonumber  \\
&  \times   \prod_{\substack{k=1 \\ k \neq i,j}}^n \{\
     (1-qu_i u_k^{-1})(1-q u_j u_k^{-1})
     \}
     F_{N,n-2,r}(\mathbf{u} \backslash \{ u_i, u_j \}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}). \label{reductionrelation}
\end{align}
<!-- end prop-8 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Since $F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$ is symmetric with respect to $\mathbf{u}$, it suffices to verify \eqref{reductionrelation} for $i=1$ and $j=2$, as illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:super}. First, we note that the $R$-matrix at the upper-left corner reduces essentially to the permutation operator \eqref{eq:Ruu}. Together with the ice rule \eqref{eq:ice-rule}, this allows the configuration of the first row (excluding the corner site) to be uniquely fixed, as shown in the middle-left panel.

To proceed, we apply the auxiliary relation depicted in Figure~\ref{fig:auxsuper}, which implies that the boundary $K$-matrix elements in the first two rows must both be $[K]_{0}^0$. Consequently, the configuration of the second row is also uniquely determined. Removing these two rows yields the smaller weight function $F_{N,n-2,r}(\mathbf{u} \setminus \{ u_1, u_2 \}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$. After multiplying by the contribution from the removed rows:
\begin{align}
    &(1-q)(\beta-\gamma u_1)(\beta-\gamma u_2) \prod_{k=1}^N \{ (1-qu_1 w_k)(1-qu_2 w_k) \}  \prod_{k=3}^n \{ (1-qu_1 u_k^{-1})(1-q u_2 u_k^{-1}) \}, \notag
\end{align}
we arrive at the reduction relation \eqref{reductionrelation}.














   

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
   \includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{superone.pdf}
 \includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{supertwo.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{superthree.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{superfour.pdf}
 \includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{superfive.pdf}
 \includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{supersix.pdf}
  \caption{The reduction \eqref{reductionrelation}
  when $i=1$, $j=2$.}
  \label{fig:super}
\end{figure}





\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
   \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{superseven.pdf}
  \caption{An auxiliary relation used in Figure~\ref{fig:super}.}
  \label{fig:auxsuper}
\end{figure}
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{susycor}
    
Assume $r\le n-2$. If $u_i u_j=1$, we have
\begin{align}
     WC_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})|_{u_i u_j=1} 
     =&(1-q) 
     \prod_{k=1}^N \{ (1-qu_i w_k)(1-qu_j w_k)  \} \nonumber \\
&   \times  \prod_{\substack{k=1 \\ k \neq i,j}}^n \{\
     (1-qu_i u_k^{-1})(1-q u_j u_k^{-1})
     \} \nonumber
     WC_{N,n-2,r}(\mathbf{u} \backslash \{ u_i, u_j \}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}), 
     \\
     WB_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})|_{u_i u_j=1} 
     =&(1-q) 
     \prod_{k=1}^N \{ (1-qu_i w_k)(1-qu_j w_k)  \} \nonumber  \\
& \times    \prod_{\substack{k=1 \\ k \neq i,j}}^n \{\
     (1-qu_i u_k^{-1})(1-q u_j u_k^{-1})
     \} \nonumber
     WB_{N,n-2,r}(\mathbf{u} \backslash \{ u_i, u_j \}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}).
\end{align}
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- prop-9: body_md -->
We have
\begin{align}
F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) 
=&
 (1-q)^{\frac{(n-r)(n-r-1)}{2}} (\beta-\gamma)^{n-r} 
 \prod_{1 \le j \le k \le r} (1-w_{m_j}^{-1} w_{m_k}^{-1})
\nonumber \\
&\times \prod_{j=1}^N (1-qw_j)^{n-r} \prod_{k=1}^r (1 - q w_{m_k}^{-1})^{n-r}  W_{N,r}(w_{m_1},\dots,w_{m_r}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}).
\label{fixedpointrelationFandWA}
\end{align}
<!-- end prop-9 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
We investigate the partition function $F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$ in its lattice-model realization. 
By leveraging its symmetry with respect to the $u$-variables, we can rearrange their assignment to the lines as shown in the top-left panel of Figure~\ref{fig:fromtri}. 
Upon setting $u_{r+1} = \dots = u_n = 1$, we observe that the configuration in the top $(n-r)$ rows is uniquely determined by the condition $[K(1)]_{0}^1 = 0$ from \eqref{eq:Ktri-components} and the ice rule \eqref{eq:ice-rule} of the $R$-matrix. 
Furthermore, the remaining part corresponds to a configuration where we can apply the same argument used to derive Lemma~\ref{factorizationlemma}. 
By collecting the corresponding $R$-matrix and $K$-matrix elements, we obtain the overall factor:
\begin{align}
& (1-q)^{\frac{(n-r)(n-r-1)}{2}} (\beta-\gamma)^{n-r} \prod_{1 \le j \le k \le r} (1-w_{m_j}^{-1} w_{m_k}^{-1})  \prod_{j=1}^N (1-qw_j)^{n-r} \prod_{k=1}^r (1 - q w_{m_k}^{-1})^{n-r}. \label{overallfactorfordiagonal}
\end{align}
The remaining part of the partition function then reduces to a rectangular shape, as depicted in the bottom panel of Figure~\ref{fig:fromtri}, which is nothing but the type $A$ partition function
$
W_{N,r}(w_{m_1},\dots,w_{m_r}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$.
Hence we obtain
\eqref{fixedpointrelationFandWA}.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Under the standing assumption $|\mathbf{M}|=|\mathbf{I}|=r$, we show that the rectangular partition functions
$
W_{N,r}(w_{m_1},\dots,w_{m_r}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$
are given by
\begin{align}
\prod_{j=1}^r \prod_{k=1}^r (1-q w_{i_k}^{-1} w_{i_j}) 
\prod_{k=1}^{r+1} \left[ \prod_{i_{k-1} < j < i_k} \left\{ \prod_{l=k}^r (1-w_{i_l}^{-1} w_j) \prod_{l=1}^{k-1} (1-q w_{i_l}^{-1} w_j) \right\} \right], \label{rectdiagonalfactor}
\end{align}
where $i_0:=0$, $i_{r+1}:=N+1$
for the case $\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{I}$, and that they vanish unless the condition $\mathbf{M} \ge \mathbf{I}$ is satisfied.

When $\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{I}$, the configuration is uniquely determined starting from the leftmost column by the ice rule \eqref{eq:ice-rule}.
For each $i_j$-th column ($j=1, \dots, r$), the configuration is depicted in the top-left panel of Figure~\ref{fig:fromtritwo}. By \eqref{eq:R-components}, these columns give the weights $\prod_{k=1}^r (1-q w_{i_k}^{-1} w_{i_j})$, resulting in the factor
\begin{equation*}
\prod_{j=1}^r \prod_{k=1}^r (1-q w_{i_k}^{-1} w_{i_j}).
\end{equation*}
For a column index $j$ such that $i_{k-1} < j < i_k$, the configuration corresponds to the top-right panel of Figure~\ref{fig:fromtritwo}. The weight contribution from such a column is $\prod_{l=k}^r (1-w_{i_l}^{-1} w_j) \prod_{l=1}^{k-1} (1-q w_{i_l}^{-1} w_j)$, which leads to the factor
\begin{align}
\prod_{k=1}^{r+1} \left[ \prod_{i_{k-1} < j < i_k} \left\{ \prod_{l=k}^r (1-w_{i_l}^{-1} w_j) \prod_{l=1}^{k-1} (1-q w_{i_l}^{-1} w_j) \right\} \right]. \notag
\end{align}
The case $k=r+1$ in the preceding product accounts for the
columns $i_r+1,\ldots,N$: the product over $l=k,\ldots,r$
is empty, and their contribution is
$\prod_{j=i_r+1}^{N}\prod_{l=1}^{r}
(1-qw_{i_l}^{-1}w_j)$.
Together with the contributions from the columns
$i_1,\ldots,i_r$, this gives \eqref{rectdiagonalfactor}.

Conversely, if the condition $\mathbf{M} \ge \mathbf{I}$ is not satisfied, let $j$ be the minimal index such that $m_j < i_j$. By analyzing the configuration from the left, we find that the $m_j$-th column must take the form shown in the bottom panel of Figure~\ref{fig:fromtritwo}. In this case, there appears an inadmissible configuration (where, by the ice rule \eqref{eq:ice-rule}, the $R$-matrix element must be zero), implying that the rectangular partition functions vanish identically.



From \eqref{fixedpointrelationFandWA} and
 \eqref{rectdiagonalfactor},
 we have
\begin{align}
&F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{I}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
=
 (1-q)^{\frac{(n-r)(n-r-1)}{2}} (\beta-\gamma)^{n-r} 
\nonumber \\
 &\times 
 \prod_{1 \le j \le k \le r} (1-w_{i_j}^{-1} w_{i_k}^{-1})
 \prod_{j=1}^N (1-qw_j)^{n-r} \prod_{k=1}^r (1 - q w_{i_k}^{-1})^{n-r} \nonumber \\
 &\times
\prod_{j=1}^r \prod_{k=1}^r (1-q w_{i_k}^{-1} w_{i_j}) \prod_{k=1}^{r+1} \left[ \prod_{i_{k-1} < j < i_k} \left\{ \prod_{l=k}^r (1-w_{i_l}^{-1} w_j) \prod_{l=1}^{k-1} (1-q w_{i_l}^{-1} w_j) \right\} \right].
\label{diagonalFfunctionanotherform}
\end{align}
A direct calculation shows that this expression is equivalent to \eqref{diagonalFfunction}.









\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{fromtrisix.pdf}
 \includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{fromtriseven.pdf}
\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{fromtrieight.pdf}
\caption{Top: Column weights for the case $\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{I}$. Bottom: Illustration of the vanishing mechanism when $\mathbf{M} \ge \mathbf{I}$ is not satisfied.}
  \label{fig:fromtritwo}
\end{figure}
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
$
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})=0$
unless $\mathbf{M} \ge \mathbf{I}$
follows from
Theorem~\ref{ffunctionproperties} $\mathrm{(i)}$.
Let us show
\eqref{normalizationWC}.
By definition \eqref{typeCnormalizeddefinition}, $\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{I}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) = \frac{WC_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{I}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})}{\mathcal{E}^C_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{I}}, \mathbf{w})}$. For brevity, we denote the complement of the index set by $\mathbf{I}^c := \{1, 2, \dots, N\} \setminus \mathbf{I}$. Executing the specialization substitution $\mathbf{u} = \mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{I}}$ in \eqref{typeCnormalizationfactor} and rearranging the equation, we obtain the following explicit form of the total normalization factor:
\begin{align}
\mathcal{E}_{N,n,r}^C(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{I}}, \mathbf{w}) =& (1-q)^{\frac{(n-r)(n-r-1)}{2}}
\prod_{j,k=1}^r (1 - q w_{i_j}^{-1} w_{i_k}) \prod_{j=1}^r (1 - q w_{i_j}^{-1})^{n-r}
\nonumber \\
&\quad \times \frac{  \prod_{k=1}^N (1 - q w_k)^{n-r} \prod_{j=1}^r \prod_{k=1}^N (1 - q w_{i_j} w_k) }{ \prod_{1 \le j < k \le r} (1 - q w_{i_j} w_{i_k}) \prod_{1 \le j \le k \le N} (1 - q w_j w_k) }.
\label{explicit_E_unified}
\end{align}

Rearranging using the expression \eqref{diagonalFfunction_rewritten} for $F_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{I}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$ and equation \eqref{explicit_E_unified} and setting $\gamma=0$, $\beta=1$, we obtain the form:
\begin{align}
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{I}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
&= \prod_{1 \le j \le k \le r} (1-w_{i_j}^{-1} w_{i_k}^{-1})  \prod_{j \in \mathbf{I}^c} \left( \prod_{l: i_l > j} (1-w_{i_l}^{-1} w_j) \prod_{l: i_l < j} (1-q w_{i_l}^{-1} w_j) \right) \nonumber \\
&\quad \times \frac{ \prod_{1 \le j < k \le r} (1 - q w_{i_j} w_{i_k}) \prod_{1 \le j \le k \le N} (1 - q w_j w_k) }{ \prod_{j=1}^r \prod_{k=1}^N (1 - q w_{i_j} w_k) }.
 \notag
\end{align}

Furthermore, simplifying using
\begin{align}
&\prod_{1 \le j \le k \le N} (1 - q w_j w_k) \nonumber \\
=& \prod_{1 \le j \le k \le r} (1 - q w_{i_j} w_{i_k})  \prod_{\substack{j \le k \\ j, 
k \in \mathbf{I}^c}} (1 - q w_j w_{k})  \prod_{j=1}^r \prod_{k \in \mathbf{I}^c} (1 - q w_{i_j} w_k),
 \notag
\end{align}
and
\begin{equation*}
\prod_{j=1}^r \prod_{k=1}^N (1 - q w_{i_j} w_k) = \prod_{j,k=1}^r (1 - q w_{i_j} w_{i_k})  \prod_{j=1}^r \prod_{k \in \mathbf{I}^c} (1 - q w_{i_j} w_k),
\end{equation*}
$\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{I}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$ reduces to the following expression:
\begin{align}
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{I}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
&=  \prod_{1 \le j \le k \le r} (1-w_{i_j}^{-1} w_{i_k}^{-1})  \prod_{j \in \mathbf{I}^c} \prod_{l: i_l > j} (1-w_{i_l}^{-1} w_j)  \nonumber \\
&\quad \times  \prod_{\substack{j \le k \\ j, k \in \mathbf{I}^c}} (1 - q w_j w_{k})  \prod_{j \in \mathbf{I}^c} \prod_{l: i_l < j} (1-q w_{i_l}^{-1} w_j) .
\label{final_explicit_blocks}
\end{align}
The expressions \eqref{typecfirstblock} and \eqref{typecsecondblock} for $\prod_{\alpha \in \operatorname{Inv}(\lambda)} (1 - e^{-\alpha})$ and $\prod_{\alpha \in T(\lambda)} (1 - q e^{+\alpha})$ of type $C$ show that the right-hand side of \eqref{final_explicit_blocks} is $\prod_{\alpha \in \operatorname{Inv}(\lambda)} (1 - e^{-\alpha}) \prod_{\alpha \in T(\lambda)} (1 - q e^{+\alpha})$.









To prove $\mathrm{(ii)}$ and $\mathrm{(iii)}$,
we use the relation
\begin{align}
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}
(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
={}&
\prod_{1\le j\le k\le r}
\left(1-w_{m_j}^{-1}w_{m_k}^{-1}\right)
\prod_{\substack{j\le k\\
j,k\in\{1,\dots,N\}\setminus\mathbf M}}
(1-qw_jw_k)
\nonumber\\
&\times
\frac{
W_{N,r}(w_{m_1},\dots,w_{m_r}|\mathbf w|\mathbf I)
}{
\displaystyle
\prod_{j,k=1}^r
(1-qw_{m_j}^{-1}w_{m_k})
}, \label{relationwithAnormalized}
\end{align}
which follows by combining \eqref{fixedpointrelationFandWA} with the normalized definition \eqref{typeCnormalizeddefinition} and the normalization factor \eqref{typeCnormalizationfactor}.
We also note the following identification with
type $A$ weight functions by Rim\'anyi--Tarasov--Varchenko:
\begin{align}
\frac{
W_{N,r}(w_{m_1},\dots,w_{m_r}|\mathbf w|\mathbf I)
}{
\displaystyle
\prod_{j,k=1}^r
(1-qw_{m_j}^{-1}w_{m_k})
}=\widetilde W_{\sigma_0,\mathcal I}^{\mathrm{RTV}}
(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}, \mathbf{w},q).
\label{identificationtypeA}
\end{align}




To prove $\mathrm{(ii)}$, we use \eqref{relationwithAnormalized}.
First note that from Theorem~\ref{RTVtheorem} $\mathrm{(i)}$,
\eqref{identificationtypeA} is divisible by
\begin{align}
e_{\sigma_0,\mathcal{M},-}^{\mathrm{vert}}
&=\prod_{\substack{j \in \mathbf{M}^c, k \in \mathbf{M} \\ \sigma_0^{-1}(j)<\sigma_0^{-1}(k)}}
(1-q w_k^{-1} w_j)=\prod_{\substack{j \in \mathbf{M}^c, k \in \mathbf{M} \\ k <j }}
(1-q w_k^{-1} w_j) \nonumber \\
&=\prod_{j \in \{1, 2, \dots, N\} \setminus \mathbf{M}} \prod_{l: m_l < j} (1-q w_{m_l}^{-1} w_j). \notag
\end{align}

It is also explicit from \eqref{relationwithAnormalized} that
$\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}
(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$
contains the two factors
\[
\prod_{1\le j\le k\le r}
(1-w_{m_j}^{-1}w_{m_k}^{-1})
\quad\text{and}\quad
\prod_{\substack{j \le k \\ j, k \in \{1, 2, \dots, N\} \setminus \mathbf{M}}}
(1-q w_j w_{k}).
\]
Since the remaining normalized type $A$ factor is divisible by the vertical product displayed above, the full product in \eqref{typeCfullDivisibility} divides
$\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$.



To prove $\mathrm{(iii)}$,
first note
that from Theorem~\ref{RTVtheorem} $\mathrm{(iii)}$
and the identification
\eqref{identificationtypeA},
we have
\begin{align}
&\varphi_{\mathbf{M}}
\Bigg(
\mathcal{N}
\Bigg(
\displaystyle
\prod_{j,k=1}^r
(1-qw_{m_j}^{-1}w_{m_k})^{-1}
W_{N,r}(w_{m_1},\dots,w_{m_r}|\mathbf w|\mathbf I)
\Bigg)
\Bigg) \nonumber \\
=&
\varphi_{\mathbf{M}} \Big(
\mathcal{N} \Big(
\widetilde W_{\sigma_0,\mathcal I}^{\mathrm{RTV}}
(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}},\mathbf{w},q)
\Big)
 \Big) \subset [0,r(N-r)-1], \label{newtfromA}
\end{align}
if $\mathbf{I} \neq \mathbf{M}$.

Using $\varphi_{\mathbf{M}} (\mathcal{N}( 1-w_{m_j}^{-1}w_{m_k}^{-1}))=[-2,0]$,
we have
\begin{align}
\varphi_{\mathbf{M}} \Bigg( \mathcal{N}
\Bigg(
\prod_{1\le j\le k\le r}
\left(1-w_{m_j}^{-1}w_{m_k}^{-1}\right)
\Bigg)
\Bigg)=[-r(r+1),0]. \label{newtfactorone}
\end{align}
A direct degree count gives
\begin{align}
\varphi_{\mathbf{M}} \Bigg( \mathcal{N}
\Bigg(
\prod_{\substack{j\le k\\
j,k\in\{1,\dots,N\}\setminus\mathbf M}}
(1-qw_jw_k)
\Bigg)
\Bigg)=[0,2(N-r)(N-r+1)]. \label{newtfactortwo}
\end{align}

From \eqref{relationwithAnormalized}, \eqref{newtfromA}, \eqref{newtfactorone},
\eqref{newtfactortwo} and the Minkowski-sum identity
$\mathcal N(fg)=\mathcal N(f)+\mathcal N(g)$, the claim follows.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- prop-10: body_md -->
Let $i \in \{1, \dots, N-1\}$.
For the normalized weight function $\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$, the following relations hold.

(i) If $i+1 = i_k \in \mathbf{I}$ and $i \notin \mathbf{I}$, we have
\begin{align}
&\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}^{(i)}) \nonumber \\
=& \frac{1}{w_i-w_{i+1}} \Big( (w_i-q w_{i+1}) \widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|s_i \mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) - (1-q)w_{i+1} \widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) \Big), \label{recrelone_tilde} \\
&\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) \nonumber \\
=& \frac{1}{q(w_i-w_{i+1})} \Big( (w_i-q w_{i+1}) \widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|s_i \mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}^{(i)}) 
- (1-q)w_{i} \widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}^{(i)}) \Big). \label{recreltwo_tilde}
\end{align}
Here $\mathbf{I}^{(i)} = \{ i_1, \dots, i_{k-1}, i, i_{k+1}, \dots, i_r \}$.

(ii) If $i,i+1 \in \mathbf{I}$ or $i,i+1 \not\in \mathbf{I}$, we have
\begin{align}
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) = \widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|s_i \mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}).
\label{recrelthree_tilde}
\end{align}
<!-- end prop-10 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
By \eqref{typeCnormalizeddefinition}, $WC_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) = \widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) \cdot \mathcal{E}_{N,n,r}^C(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})$.
We check the action of the simple transposition $s_i$ (swapping $w_i$ and $w_{i+1}$) on the normalization factor $\mathcal{E}^C_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})$.
By \eqref{typeCnormalizationfactor}, the variable $\mathbf{w}$ in $\mathcal{E}^C_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})$ appears only in the products $\prod_{j=1}^n \prod_{k=1}^N (1 - q u_j w_k)$ and $\prod_{1 \le j \le k \le N} (1 - q w_j w_k)$.
Since these are both symmetric products over all $k \in \{1, \dots, N\}$, they are invariant under the action of any simple transposition $s_i$. Thus,
$
\mathcal{E}^C_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}, s_i \mathbf{w}) = \mathcal{E}^C_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w}).
$
Therefore, substituting $WC_{N,n,r} = \widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r} \cdot \mathcal{E}^C_{N,n,r}$ into the recursive relations of the
$\gamma=0$, $\beta=1$ case of
Proposition~\ref{diddiffbeforenorm}, the invariant factor $\mathcal{E}^C_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})$ completely cancels from both sides of all equations, so $\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}$ satisfies the same recursive relations as the original $WC_{N,n,r}$.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- prop-11: body_md -->
The normalized weight function $\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}$ satisfies the following boundary recursive relations.

(i) If $1 \in \mathbf{I}$, we have
\begin{align}
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r-1}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}^{(0)})
=& \frac{1}{1-w_1^2} \Bigg( (1 - q w_1^2) \widetilde{WC}_{N,n+1,r}(\mathbf{u}, w_1^{-1}|w_1^{-1}, w_2, \dotsm, w_N|\mathbf{I})
\nonumber \\
 &- (1-q) w_1^2 \widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) \Bigg), \label{pizerorecursionone_tilde}
\end{align}
Here $\mathbf{I}^{(0)}=\{ i_2,\dotsm,i_r \}$.

(ii) If $1 \not\in \mathbf{I}$, we have
\begin{align}
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n+1,r}(\mathbf{u}, w_1^{-1}|w_1^{-1}, w_2, \dotsm, w_N|\mathbf{I})
= \frac{1-q}{1 - q w_1^2} \widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}). \label{pizerorecursiontwo_tilde}
\end{align}
<!-- end prop-11 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
For brevity, we write $\mathbf{u}' = (\mathbf{u}, w_1^{-1})$ and \\ $\mathbf{w}' = (w_1^{-1}, w_2, \dotsm, w_N)$ in the proof.
From \eqref{typeCnormalizationfactor}, the ratio under the parameter shift $(\mathbf{u}', \mathbf{w}')$ is
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathcal{E}_{N,n+1,r}^C(\mathbf{u}', \mathbf{w}')}{\mathcal{E}_{N,n,r}^C(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})}
&= (1-q w_1^2)  \prod_{j=1}^n (1 - q w_1^{-1} u_j^{-1}) \prod_{k=2}^N (1 - q w_1 w_k).
\label{E_ratio_shift}
\end{align}
Also, regarding the case where the index $r$ changes to $r-1$, since only the exponent of the coefficient part changes, we immediately obtain the following:
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathcal{E}_{N,n,r}^C(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})}{\mathcal{E}^C_{N,n,r-1}(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})}  = 1-q.
\label{E_ratio_r}
\end{align}

To prove (i), we substitute $WC_{N,n,r} = \widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r} \cdot \mathcal{E}_{N,n,r}^C$ into the original relation $\gamma=0$, $\beta=1$ of \eqref{pizerorecursionone} and divide both sides by $\mathcal{E}_{N,n,r-1}^C(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})$:
\begin{align*}
&\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r-1}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}^{(0)}) \nonumber \\
&= \frac{1}{1-w_1^2} \Bigg( (1-q)^{-1} \prod_{j=1}^n (1-q w_1^{-1} u_j^{-1})^{-1} \prod_{k=2}^{N} (1-q w_1 w_k)^{-1} \frac{\mathcal{E}_{N,n+1,r}^C(\mathbf{u}', \mathbf{w}')}{\mathcal{E}_{N,n,r-1}^C(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})} 
\nonumber \\
&\times \widetilde{WC}_{N,n+1,r}(\mathbf{u}'|\mathbf{w}'|\mathbf{I})  -  w_1^2 \frac{\mathcal{E}_{N,n,r}^C(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})}{\mathcal{E}_{N,n,r-1}^C(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})} \widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) \Bigg).
\end{align*}
Decomposing the ratio of the normalization factors 
in the first term as \\
$\frac{\mathcal{E}^C_{N,n+1,r}(\mathbf{u}', \mathbf{w}')}{\mathcal{E}^C_{N,n,r-1}(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})} = \frac{\mathcal{E}_{N,n+1,r}^C(\mathbf{u}', \mathbf{w}')}{\mathcal{E}^C_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})} \cdot \frac{\mathcal{E}^C_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})}{\mathcal{E}^C_{N,n,r-1}(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})}$ and applying equations \eqref{E_ratio_shift} and \eqref{E_ratio_r}, equation \eqref{pizerorecursionone_tilde} is derived.

To prove (ii), we substitute $WC_{N,n,r} = \widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r} \cdot \mathcal{E}_{N,n,r}^C$ into the original relation $\gamma=0$, $\beta=1$ of
\eqref{pizerorecursiontwo} and divide both sides by $\mathcal{E}_{N,n+1,r}^C(\mathbf{u}', \mathbf{w}')$. This yields the following:
\begin{align*}
&\widetilde{WC}_{N,n+1,r}(\mathbf{u}'|\mathbf{w}'|\mathbf{I}) \nonumber \\
=& (1-q)  \prod_{j=1}^n (1-q w_1^{-1} u_j^{-1}) \prod_{k=2}^{N} (1-q w_1 w_k) \cdot \frac{\mathcal{E}^C_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{w})}{\mathcal{E}_{N,n+1,r}^C(\mathbf{u}', \mathbf{w}')} \widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}).
\end{align*}
Substituting the inverse of the ratio \eqref{E_ratio_shift} into this equation derives equation \eqref{pizerorecursiontwo_tilde}.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- prop-12: body_md -->
Assume $r\le n-2$. We have
\begin{align}
 \widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})\Big|_{u_i u_j = 1}& = \frac{(1-q)^2}{(1-qu_i^2)(1-qu_j^2)} \widetilde{WC}_{N,n-2,r}(\mathbf{u} \setminus \{u_i, u_j\}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}). \label{normalizedsusytwo} 
\end{align}
<!-- end prop-12 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Direct substitution in \eqref{typeCnormalizationfactor} gives
\begin{align}
    &\frac{\mathcal{E}_{N,n,r}^C(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})|_{u_i u_j = 1}}{\mathcal{E}_{N,n-2,r}^C(\mathbf{u} \setminus \{u_i, u_j\}, \mathbf{w})} 
    = \frac{1}{1-q}(1-qu_i^2)(1-qu_j^2) 
   \nonumber \\ 
&\times    \prod_{k=1}^N (1-qu_iw_k)(1-qu_jw_k)  \prod_{\substack{k=1 \\ k \neq i,j}}^n (1-qu_k^{-1}u_i)(1-qu_k^{-1}u_j). \notag
\end{align}
Combining with $\gamma=0$, $\beta=1$ of
\eqref{reductionrelation},
we obtain \eqref{normalizedsusytwo}.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- prop-13: body_md -->
Assume $r\le n-1$. We have
\begin{align}
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})\Big|_{u_n = 1}& =\widetilde{WC}_{N,n-1,r}(\mathbf{u} \setminus \{ u_n \}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}).
\label{stabilityWC}
\end{align}
<!-- end prop-13 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Lemma~\ref{onevariablesp} gives \eqref{Fonevariablesp}.
Direct substitution in \eqref{typeCnormalizationfactor} gives
\begin{align}
\left.
\mathcal{E}_{N,n,r}^{C}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})
\right|_{u_n=1}
&=
\prod_{k=1}^{N}(1-qw_k)
\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}(1-qu_k^{-1})
\,\mathcal{E}^{C}_{N,n-1,r}(\mathbf{u}\setminus\{u_n\},\mathbf{w}). \notag
\end{align}
Combining with $\gamma=0, \beta=1$ of
\eqref{Fonevariablesp}, we obtain \eqref{stabilityWC}.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- prop-14: body_md -->
\label{prop:B_si}
Let $i \in \{1, \dotsm, N-1\}$. For the normalized weight function $\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$, the following relations hold.

(i) If $i+1 = i_k \in \mathbf{I}$ and $i \notin \mathbf{I}$, we have
\begin{align}
&\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}^{(i)}) \nonumber \\
=& \frac{1}{w_i-w_{i+1}} \Big( (w_i-q w_{i+1}) \widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|s_i \mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) - (1-q)w_{i+1} \widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) \Big), \\
&\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) \nonumber \\
=& \frac{1}{q(w_i-w_{i+1})} \Big( (w_i-q w_{i+1}) \widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|s_i \mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}^{(i)}) - (1-q)w_{i} \widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}^{(i)}) \Big),
\end{align}
here $\mathbf{I}^{(i)} = \{ i_1, \dotsm, i_{k-1}, i, i_{k+1}, \dotsm, i_r \}$.

(ii) If $i,i+1 \in \mathbf{I}$ or $i,i+1 \not\in \mathbf{I}$, we have
\begin{align}
\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) = \widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|s_i \mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}).
\end{align}
<!-- end prop-14 -->
<!-- prop-15: body_md -->
\label{prop:B_shift}
The normalized weight function $\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}$ satisfies the following boundary recursive relations.

(i) If $1 \in \mathbf{I}$, we have
\begin{align}
\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r-1}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}^{(0)}) =& \frac{1}{1-w_1} \Bigg( (1 - q w_1) \widetilde{WB}_{N,n+1,r}(\mathbf{u}, w_1^{-1}|w_1^{-1}, w_2, \dotsm, w_N|\mathbf{I}) \nonumber \\
&- (1-q) w_1 \widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}) \Bigg),
\end{align}
here $\mathbf{I}^{(0)}=\{ i_2,\dotsm,i_r \}$.

(ii) If $1 \not\in \mathbf{I}$, we have
\begin{align}
\widetilde{WB}_{N,n+1,r}(\mathbf{u}, w_1^{-1}|w_1^{-1}, w_2, \dotsm, w_N|\mathbf{I}) = \frac{1-q}{1-qw_1} \widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}).
\end{align}
<!-- end prop-15 -->
<!-- prop-16: body_md -->
Assume $r\le n-2$. We have
\begin{align}
 \widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})\Big|_{u_i u_j = 1}& = \frac{(1-q)^2}{(1-qu_i)(1-qu_j)} \widetilde{WB}_{N,n-2,r}(\mathbf{u} \setminus \{u_i, u_j\}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}). \label{normalizedsusythree}
\end{align}
<!-- end prop-16 -->
<!-- prop-17: body_md -->
Assume $r\le n-1$. We have
\begin{align}
\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})\Big|_{u_n = 1}& =\widetilde{WB}_{N,n-1,r}(\mathbf{u} \setminus \{ u_n \}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}).
\label{stabilityWB}
\end{align}
<!-- end prop-17 -->
<!-- prop-18: body_md -->
If $|\mathbf{M}|<|\mathbf{I}|=r$, then
\begin{equation}
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})=0.
\label{eq:typeC-lower-cardinality-vanishing}
\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-18 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
By \eqref{eq:typeC-stability-s-lt-r}, it is enough to consider
$\widetilde{WC}_{N,r,r}$ at a specialization containing at least one variable
$u_j=1$.  The factorization \eqref{typeCnrFactorization} shows that the
corresponding unnormalized function $WC_{N,r,r}$ vanishes because it contains
the factor $1-u_j^{-2}$.  By the normalized definition
\eqref{typeCnormalizeddefinition} and the normalization factor
\eqref{typeCnormalizationfactor}, $\mathcal E^C_{N,r,r}$ is finite and
nonzero at this specialization for generic parameters.  Hence the normalized
value also vanishes.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- thm-5: body_md -->
For every $s=|\mathbf{M}|$ with $r=|\mathbf{I}|\le s\le n$,
\begin{align}
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
&=(1-q)^{s-r}
\prod_{\substack{j\le k\\j,k\in\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}}
(1-qw_jw_k)
\sum_{\substack{\mathbf{K}\subset\mathbf{M}\\|\mathbf{K}|=r}}
\frac{
W_{N,r}(w_{k_1},\ldots,w_{k_r}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
}{
\displaystyle
\prod_{a,b=1}^{r}(1-qw_{k_a}^{-1}w_{k_b})
}
\notag\\
&\quad\times
\frac{
\displaystyle
\prod_{1\le a\le b\le r}
(1-w_{k_a}^{-1}w_{k_b}^{-1})
\prod_{a=1}^{r}\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}
(1-w_{k_a}^{-1}w_{l_b}^{-1})
}{
\displaystyle
\prod_{a=1}^{r}\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}
(1-w_{k_a}^{-1}w_{l_b})
}
\notag\\
&\quad\times
\prod_{1\le a<b\le s-r}
(1-qw_{l_a}^{-1}w_{l_b}^{-1})
\prod_{a=1}^{s-r}
\prod_{j\in\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}
(1-qw_{l_a}^{-1}w_j).
\label{eq:typeC-cross-sector-branching}
\end{align}
For $r=0$, set $W_{N,0}=1$, and interpret every empty product as $1$.
<!-- end thm-5 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
By \eqref{eq:typeC-stability-s-ge-r},
$
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}
(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
=
\widetilde{WC}_{N,s,r}
(w_{m_1},\ldots,w_{m_s}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I}).
$
Therefore it is enough to decompose
$WC_{N,s,r}(w_{m_1},\ldots,w_{m_s}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})$.
Fix
$
\mathbf{K}=\{k_1<\cdots<k_r\}\subset\mathbf{M}$
and $
\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{M}\setminus\mathbf{K}
=\{l_1<\cdots<l_{s-r}\}.
$
By the definition \eqref{WCfunction},
$WC_{N,s,r}$ is obtained from the explicit formula \eqref{Motherfunction}
for $F_{N,s,r}$ by setting $\gamma=0$ and $\beta=1$.
We apply this specialized form of \eqref{Motherfunction} and collect the
permutations for which the variables in the first $r$ positions are precisely
$w_{k_1},\ldots,w_{k_r}$ and those in the remaining $s-r$ positions are
$w_{l_1},\ldots,w_{l_{s-r}}$.  The factors coupling these two sets are
invariant under permutations within either set.  Consequently, before any
further simplification, the specialized form of \eqref{Motherfunction} can be
written as the following sum over subsets:
\begin{align}
&WC_{N,s,r}(w_{m_1},\ldots,w_{m_s}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
\notag\\
&=\frac{1}{(s-r)!}
\sum_{\substack{\mathbf{K}\subset\mathbf{M}\\|\mathbf{K}|=r}}
\prod_{a=1}^{r}w_{k_a}^{-(N+s)}
\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}w_{l_b}^{-(N+s)}
\notag\\
&\quad\times
\sum_{\sigma\in S_r}\sigma\Bigg[
\prod_{j=1}^{r}
\left\{
(1-q)(u_j^2-1)
\prod_{c=i_j+1}^{N}(u_j-qw_c)
\prod_{c=1}^{i_j-1}(u_j-w_c)
\right\}
\notag\\
&\hspace{40mm}\times
\prod_{1\le j<k\le r}
\frac{qu_j-u_k}{u_j-u_k}(u_ju_k-1)
\Bigg]_{(u_1,\ldots,u_r)=(w_{k_1},\ldots,w_{k_r})}
\notag\\
&\quad\times
\sum_{\rho\in S_{s-r}}\rho\Bigg[
\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}
\left\{
 u_{r+b}\prod_{c=1}^{N}(u_{r+b}-qw_c)
\right\}
\notag\\
&\quad\times
\prod_{a=1}^{r}\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}
\frac{qu_{r+b}-w_{k_a}}{u_{r+b}-w_{k_a}}
(w_{k_a}u_{r+b}-1)
\prod_{1\le a<b\le s-r}(u_{r+a}u_{r+b}-q)
\Bigg]_{\substack{
(u_{r+1},\ldots,u_s)\\
=(w_{l_1},\ldots,w_{l_{s-r}})}}.
\label{eq:typeC-subset-sum-before-grouping}
\end{align}
We first evaluate the sum over $\rho$.  The action of $\rho$ permutes only
$u_{r+1},\ldots,u_s$.  Since each of the three products below is symmetric in
these variables, all $(s-r)!$ terms are equal.  Therefore
\begin{align}
&\frac{1}{(s-r)!}
\sum_{\rho\in S_{s-r}}\rho\Bigg[
\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}
\left\{
 u_{r+b}\prod_{c=1}^{N}(u_{r+b}-qw_c)
\right\}
\notag\\
&\times
\prod_{a=1}^{r}\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}
\frac{qu_{r+b}-w_{k_a}}{u_{r+b}-w_{k_a}}
(w_{k_a}u_{r+b}-1)
\prod_{1\le a<b\le s-r}(u_{r+a}u_{r+b}-q)
\Bigg]_{\substack{
(u_{r+1},\ldots,u_s)\\
=(w_{l_1},\ldots,w_{l_{s-r}})}}
\notag\\
&=
\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}
\left\{
 w_{l_b}\prod_{c=1}^{N}(w_{l_b}-qw_c)
\right\}
\prod_{a=1}^{r}\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}
\frac{qw_{l_b}-w_{k_a}}{w_{l_b}-w_{k_a}}
(w_{k_a}w_{l_b}-1)
\prod_{1\le a<b\le s-r}(w_{l_a}w_{l_b}-q).
 \notag
\end{align}
Thus the subset decomposition
\eqref{eq:typeC-subset-sum-before-grouping} becomes the following completely
explicit formula:
\begin{align}
&WC_{N,s,r}(w_{m_1},\ldots,w_{m_s}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
=
\sum_{\substack{\mathbf{K}\subset\mathbf{M}\\|\mathbf{K}|=r}}
\prod_{a=1}^{r}w_{k_a}^{-(N+s)}
\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}w_{l_b}^{-(N+s)}
\notag\\
&\quad\times
\sum_{\sigma\in S_r}\sigma\Bigg[
\prod_{j=1}^{r}
\left\{
(1-q)(u_j^2-1)
\prod_{c=i_j+1}^{N}(u_j-qw_c)
\prod_{c=1}^{i_j-1}(u_j-w_c)
\right\}
\notag\\
&\quad\times
\prod_{1\le j<k\le r}
\frac{qu_j-u_k}{u_j-u_k}(u_ju_k-1)
\Bigg]_{(u_1,\ldots,u_r)=(w_{k_1},\ldots,w_{k_r})}
\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}
\left\{
w_{l_b}\prod_{c=1}^{N}(w_{l_b}-qw_c)
\right\}
\notag\\
&\quad\times
\prod_{a=1}^{r}\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}
\frac{qw_{l_b}-w_{k_a}}{w_{l_b}-w_{k_a}}
(w_{k_a}w_{l_b}-1)
\prod_{1\le a<b\le s-r}(w_{l_a}w_{l_b}-q).
\label{eq:typeC-subset-sum-after-rho}
\end{align}
We next identify the $\sigma$-sum in each summand of
\eqref{eq:typeC-subset-sum-after-rho}.  Applying \eqref{Motherfunction}
with $n=r$, $\gamma=0$, and $\beta=1$, and then using the definition
\eqref{WCfunction},
\begin{align}
&\prod_{a=1}^{r}w_{k_a}^{-(N+s)}
\sum_{\sigma\in S_r}\sigma\Bigg[
\prod_{j=1}^{r}
\left\{
(1-q)(u_j^2-1)
\prod_{c=i_j+1}^{N}(u_j-qw_c)
\prod_{c=1}^{i_j-1}(u_j-w_c)
\right\}
\notag\\
&\hspace{34mm}\times
\prod_{1\le j<k\le r}
\frac{qu_j-u_k}{u_j-u_k}(u_ju_k-1)
\Bigg]_{(u_1,\ldots,u_r)=(w_{k_1},\ldots,w_{k_r})}
\notag\\
&\qquad=
WC_{N,r,r}
(w_{k_1},\ldots,w_{k_r}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
\prod_{a=1}^{r}w_{k_a}^{-(s-r)}.
\label{eq:typeC-first-r-contribution}
\end{align}
Indeed, the monomial prefactor in $WC_{N,r,r}$ is
$\prod_{a=1}^{r}w_{k_a}^{-(N+r)}$, whereas the monomial prefactor inherited
from $WC_{N,s,r}$ is $\prod_{a=1}^{r}w_{k_a}^{-(N+s)}$.
Substituting~\eqref{eq:typeC-first-r-contribution} into
\eqref{eq:typeC-subset-sum-after-rho}, we obtain
\begin{align}
&WC_{N,s,r}(w_{m_1},\ldots,w_{m_s}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
\notag\\
&=
\sum_{\substack{\mathbf{K}\subset\mathbf{M}\\|\mathbf{K}|=r}}
WC_{N,r,r}(w_{k_1},\ldots,w_{k_r}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
\prod_{a=1}^{r}w_{k_a}^{-(s-r)}
\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}w_{l_b}^{-(N+s)}
\notag\\
&\quad\times
\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}
\left\{
w_{l_b}\prod_{c=1}^{N}(w_{l_b}-qw_c)
\right\}
\prod_{a=1}^{r}\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}
\frac{qw_{l_b}-w_{k_a}}{w_{l_b}-w_{k_a}}
(w_{k_a}w_{l_b}-1)
\prod_{1\le a<b\le s-r}(w_{l_a}w_{l_b}-q).
\label{eq:typeC-sum-through-WCrr}
\end{align}

We use
\begin{align}
&w_{l_b}^{-(N+s)}w_{l_b}
\prod_{c=1}^{N}(w_{l_b}-qw_c)
=
w_{l_b}^{1-s}
\prod_{k=1}^{N}(1-qw_{l_b}^{-1}w_k),
 \notag\\
&\frac{qw_{l_b}-w_{k_a}}{w_{l_b}-w_{k_a}}
(w_{k_a}w_{l_b}-1)
=
w_{k_a}w_{l_b}
\frac{
(1-qw_{k_a}^{-1}w_{l_b})
(1-w_{k_a}^{-1}w_{l_b}^{-1})
}{
1-w_{k_a}^{-1}w_{l_b}
},
 \notag\\
&w_{l_a}w_{l_b}-q
=
w_{l_a}w_{l_b}
(1-qw_{l_a}^{-1}w_{l_b}^{-1}).
 \notag
\end{align}

Applying~\eqref{relationwithAnormalized} with $n=r$ and $\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{K}$, and then using the normalized definition \eqref{typeCnormalizeddefinition}, gives
\begin{align}
&WC_{N,r,r}(w_{k_1},\ldots,w_{k_r}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
\notag\\
&=
\mathcal E^C_{N,r,r}(w_{k_1},\ldots,w_{k_r},\mathbf{w})
\prod_{1\le a\le b\le r}
(1-w_{k_a}^{-1}w_{k_b}^{-1})
\prod_{\substack{j\le k\\j,k\in\mathbf{K}^{\mathrm c}}}
(1-qw_jw_k)
\frac{
W_{N,r}(w_{k_1},\ldots,w_{k_r}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
}{
\displaystyle
\prod_{a,b=1}^{r}(1-qw_{k_a}^{-1}w_{k_b})
}.
 \notag
\end{align}
Here $\mathbf{K}^{\mathrm c}=\{1,\dots,N\}\setminus\mathbf{K}$.

Moreover,
\begin{align}
\frac{
\mathcal E^C_{N,r,r}(w_{k_1},\ldots,w_{k_r},\mathbf{w})
}{
\mathcal E^C_{N,s,r}(w_{m_1},\ldots,w_{m_s},\mathbf{w})
}
&=(1-q)^{s-r}
\frac{
\displaystyle
\prod_{a=1}^{r}\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}
(1-qw_{k_a}w_{l_b})
\prod_{1\le a<b\le s-r}
(1-qw_{l_a}w_{l_b})
}{
\displaystyle
\prod_{a=1}^{r}\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}
(1-qw_{k_a}^{-1}w_{l_b})
\prod_{a=1}^{s-r}\prod_{b=1}^{r}
(1-qw_{l_a}^{-1}w_{k_b})
}
\notag\\
&\qquad\times
\frac{1}{
\displaystyle
\prod_{a,b=1}^{s-r}(1-qw_{l_a}^{-1}w_{l_b})
\prod_{a=1}^{s-r}\prod_{j=1}^{N}(1-qw_{l_a}w_j)
}.
 \notag
\end{align}

Substituting these identities into the summand of
\eqref{eq:typeC-sum-through-WCrr} indexed by $\mathbf{K}$, and then dividing
by the normalization factor
$
\mathcal E^C_{N,s,r}(w_{m_1},\ldots,w_{m_s},\mathbf{w}),
$
we obtain
\begin{align}
&(1-q)^{s-r}
\prod_{\substack{j\le k\\j,k\in\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}}
(1-qw_jw_k)
\frac{
W_{N,r}(w_{k_1},\ldots,w_{k_r}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
}{
\displaystyle
\prod_{a,b=1}^{r}(1-qw_{k_a}^{-1}w_{k_b})
}
\notag\\
&\quad\times
\frac{
\displaystyle
\prod_{1\le a\le b\le r}
(1-w_{k_a}^{-1}w_{k_b}^{-1})
\prod_{a=1}^{r}\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}
(1-w_{k_a}^{-1}w_{l_b}^{-1})
}{
\displaystyle
\prod_{a=1}^{r}\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}
(1-w_{k_a}^{-1}w_{l_b})
}
\notag\\
&\quad\times
\prod_{1\le a<b\le s-r}
(1-qw_{l_a}^{-1}w_{l_b}^{-1})
\prod_{a=1}^{s-r}
\prod_{j\in\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}
(1-qw_{l_a}^{-1}w_j).
\label{eq:typeC-one-branch-explicit}
\end{align}
Summing~\eqref{eq:typeC-one-branch-explicit} over all
$\mathbf{K}\subset\mathbf{M}$ with $|\mathbf{K}|=r$ proves the formula.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- prop-19: body_md -->
Let $s=|\mathbf{M}|$ and assume $r\le s\le n$. Then
\begin{equation*}
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})\ne0
\end{equation*}
only if
\begin{equation}
i_j\le m_{s-r+j}
\qquad(j=1,\ldots,r).
\label{eq:typeC-shifted-dominance}
\end{equation}
This is equivalent to $\lambda(\mathbf{I})\subseteq\lambda(\mathbf{M})$.
<!-- end prop-19 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
The $\mathbf{K}$-term of~\eqref{eq:typeC-cross-sector-branching} contains
\[
\frac{W_{N,r}(w_{k_1},\ldots,w_{k_r}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})}
{\displaystyle\prod_{a,b=1}^{r}(1-qw_{k_a}^{-1}w_{k_b})}.
\]
By type $A$ triangularity (see the argument following \eqref{rectdiagonalfactor}), this factor vanishes unless $\mathbf{K}\ge \mathbf{I}$.
The necessary and sufficient condition for $\mathbf{M}$ to contain an $r$-element subset $\mathbf{K}\ge \mathbf{I}$ is
$
\{m_{s-r+1},\ldots,m_s\}\ge \mathbf{I}.
$
This is precisely~\eqref{eq:typeC-shifted-dominance}.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- prop-20: body_md -->
For every $\mathbf{M}=\{m_1<\cdots<m_s\}\subset\{1,\dots,N\}$ with $s\le n$,
\begin{equation}
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
\in
\mathbb{C}(q)[w_1^{\pm1},\ldots,w_N^{\pm1}].
\label{eq:typeC-laurent-polynomiality}
\end{equation}
Moreover, this Laurent polynomial is divisible by
\begin{equation}
\prod_{\substack{j\le k\\j,k\in\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}}
(1-qw_jw_k).
\label{eq:typeC-complement-factor-general-s}
\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-20 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
If $|\mathbf{M}|<r$, the value is zero by~\eqref{eq:typeC-lower-cardinality-vanishing}.
Assume henceforth that $s=|\mathbf{M}|\ge r$, and set
\[
X_{\mathbf{M}}:=\operatorname{Gr}
\left(r,\bigoplus_{j=1}^{s}\mathbb{C}_{w_{m_j}}\right).
\]
Here $\mathbb{C}_{w_{m_j}}$ is the one-dimensional $T$-representation of weight
$w_{m_j}$. Denote the tautological and quotient bundles by
$\mathscr S$ and $\mathscr Q$, respectively.
At the fixed point
$\mathbf{K}=\{k_1<\cdots<k_r\}$, write
$
\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{M}\setminus\mathbf{K}
=\{l_1<\cdots<l_{s-r}\}.
$
The weights of $\mathscr S$ are $w_{k_1},\ldots,w_{k_r}$, and the weights of
$\mathscr Q$ are $w_{l_1},\ldots,w_{l_{s-r}}$.

The factor
\[
\frac{
W_{N,r}(w_{k_1},\ldots,w_{k_r}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
}{
\displaystyle
\prod_{a,b=1}^{r}(1-qw_{k_a}^{-1}w_{k_b})
}
\]
is the restriction to $X_{\mathbf{M}}$ of the normalized type $A$ weight function class~\cite{RTV}, using
$K_T(T^*\operatorname{Gr}(r,N))\simeq K_T(\operatorname{Gr}(r,N))$.
By~\eqref{eq:typeC-lambda-y-weights}, the remaining numerator products are
\begin{align}
&\prod_{1\le a\le b\le r}
(1-w_{k_a}^{-1}w_{k_b}^{-1})
=
\lambda_{-1}(\operatorname{Sym}^2\mathscr S^*)|_{\mathbf{K}},
\notag\\
&\prod_{a=1}^{r}\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}
(1-w_{k_a}^{-1}w_{l_b}^{-1})
=
\lambda_{-1}(\mathscr S^*\otimes\mathscr Q^*)|_{\mathbf{K}},
\notag\\
&\prod_{1\le a<b\le s-r}
(1-qw_{l_a}^{-1}w_{l_b}^{-1})
=
\lambda_{-q}(\bigwedge^2\mathscr Q^*)|_{\mathbf{K}},
\notag\\
&\prod_{a=1}^{s-r}\prod_{j\in\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}
(1-qw_{l_a}^{-1}w_j)
=
\lambda_{-q}
\left(
\mathscr Q^*\otimes
\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}\mathbb{C}_{w_k}
\right) \Bigg|_{\mathbf{K}}.
 \notag
\end{align}
Thus the numerator is the fixed-point restriction of a single global equivariant
$K$-class.

For the denominator,
\begin{align}
&\prod_{a=1}^{r}\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}
(1-w_{k_a}^{-1}w_{l_b})
=(-1)^{r(s-r)}
\frac{(\det\mathscr Q|_{\mathbf{K}})^r}
{(\det\mathscr S|_{\mathbf{K}})^{s-r}}
\lambda_{-1}(T_{\mathbf{K}}^*X_{\mathbf{M}}).
\label{eq:typeC-euler-denominator}
\end{align}
Therefore, the fixed-point sum in~\eqref{eq:typeC-cross-sector-branching} is the
pushforward of a single equivariant $K$-class with a line-bundle twist.
Its value lies in the representation ring and is therefore a Laurent polynomial.
The divisibility follows from the factor outside the sum in
\eqref{eq:typeC-cross-sector-branching}.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- prop-21: body_md -->
For every $s=|\mathbf{M}|$ with $r\le s\le n$,
\begin{equation}
\varphi_{\mathbf{M}}\left(
\mathcal N\left(
WC_{N,s,r}(w_{m_1},\ldots,w_{m_s}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
\right)
\right)
\subset
[-s^2+s-2r,\ s(N-s)-r+|\mathbf{I}\cap \mathbf{M}|].
\label{eq:typeC-raw-newton-bound}
\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-21 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
By \eqref{WCfunction}, we use \eqref{Motherfunction} with $n=s$ and
$\gamma=0$, $\beta=1$. After specializing
$(u_1,\ldots,u_s)=(w_{m_1},\ldots,w_{m_s})$, each $u_j$ occurring in every
summand of this specialized formula is one of
$w_{m_1},\ldots,w_{m_s}$. Hence $\deg_{\mathbf M}u_j=1$ for $j=1,\ldots,s$.

It follows that $u_j^2-1$ has degree interval $[0,2]$. Each factor of the form $u_j-qw_k$ or $u_j-w_k$ is homogeneous of degree $1$ when $k\in\mathbf{M}$, while its two terms have degrees $1$ and $2$ when $k\notin\mathbf{M}$. Moreover, each quotient $(qu_j-u_k)/(u_j-u_k)$ is homogeneous of degree $0$, since its numerator and denominator are homogeneous of the same degree. Finally, each factor $u_ju_k-1$ or $u_ju_k-q$ has degree interval $[0,2]$.

We now apply these observations to the four parts of a fixed summand of the
specialized form of \eqref{Motherfunction}. First, the monomial prefactor
$\prod_{j=1}^{s}u_j^{-(N+s)}$ has the single degree $-s(N+s)$, and hence
degree interval $[-s(N+s),-s(N+s)]$.

Next, fix $j\in\{1,\ldots,r\}$ and consider
\[
(u_j^2-1)
\prod_{k=i_j+1}^{N}(u_j-qw_k)
\prod_{k=1}^{i_j-1}(u_j-w_k).
\]
There are $N-1$ factors of the form $u_j-qw_k$ or $u_j-w_k$, since the factor with $k=i_j$ is omitted. Every one of these factors contributes at least degree $1$, so the lower endpoint is $N-1$.

Among the indices $k\ne i_j$, the number of indices outside $\mathbf{M}$ is
\[
\begin{cases}
N-s, & i_j\in\mathbf{M},\\
N-s-1, & i_j\notin\mathbf{M}.
\end{cases}
\]
Indeed, if $i_j\in\mathbf{M}$, the omitted index $i_j$ is not one of the
$N-s$ indices outside $\mathbf{M}$, whereas if $i_j\notin\mathbf{M}$, one of
those $N-s$ indices is omitted. Each remaining index outside $\mathbf{M}$ can
increase the upper degree by one, and the factor $u_j^2-1$ contributes at most
degree $2$. Therefore the upper endpoint is
\[
\begin{cases}
2N-s+1, & i_j\in\mathbf{M},\\
2N-s, & i_j\notin\mathbf{M}.
\end{cases}
\]
When these contributions are multiplied over $j=1,\ldots,r$, the first case
occurs exactly $|\mathbf{I}\cap\mathbf{M}|$ times. Hence the upper endpoint
of the product is
\[
r(2N-s)+|\mathbf{I}\cap\mathbf{M}|.
\]
Thus we obtain
\begin{align}
&\prod_{j=1}^{r}
\left\{
(u_j^2-1)
\prod_{k=i_j+1}^{N}(u_j-qw_k)
\prod_{k=1}^{i_j-1}(u_j-w_k)
\right\} \notag
\end{align}
has degree interval contained in
$[r(N-1),\,r(2N-s)+|\mathbf{I}\cap\mathbf{M}|]$.


For each $j=r+1,\ldots,s$, consider $u_j\prod_{k=1}^{N}(u_j-qw_k)$. The factor $u_j$ has degree $1$. Among the other $N$ factors, the $s$ factors indexed by $k\in\mathbf{M}$ are homogeneous of degree $1$, whereas the $N-s$ factors indexed by $k\notin\mathbf{M}$ have degree interval $[1,2]$. Hence the degree interval for one such $j$ is contained in $[1+N,1+s+2(N-s)]=[N+1,2N-s+1]$. Multiplying over $j=r+1,\ldots,s$ gives the degree interval $[(s-r)(N+1),(s-r)(2N-s+1)]$.

It remains to consider the factors involving two $u$-variables:
\begin{align*}
&\prod_{1\le j<k\le r}
\frac{qu_j-u_k}{u_j-u_k}(u_ju_k-1)
\prod_{j=r+1}^{s}\prod_{k=1}^{r}
\frac{qu_j-u_k}{u_j-u_k}(u_ju_k-1)
\prod_{r+1\le j<k\le s}(u_ju_k-q).
\end{align*}
Every quotient has degree $0$, while every remaining factor has degree interval $[0,2]$. The total number of these remaining factors is $\binom r2+r(s-r)+\binom{s-r}{2}=\binom s2$. Consequently, their product has degree interval $\Big[0,2\binom s2 \Big]=[0,s(s-1)]$.

Adding the lower endpoints gives $-s(N+s)+r(N-1)+(s-r)(N+1)=-s^2+s-2r$. Adding the upper endpoints gives
\begin{align*}
&-s(N+s)+r(2N-s)+|\mathbf{I}\cap\mathbf{M}|
 +(s-r)(2N-s+1)+s(s-1) \notag \\
=&s(N-s)-r+|\mathbf{I}\cap\mathbf{M}|.
\end{align*}
Thus each summand is a finite sum of homogeneous rational functions of degrees between $-s^2+s-2r$ and $s(N-s)-r+|\mathbf{I}\cap\mathbf{M}|$. Since the sum over the permutations in the specialized form of \eqref{Motherfunction} is a Laurent polynomial, its homogeneous components lie in the same range. Therefore its degree interval is contained in $[-s^2+s-2r,\,s(N-s)-r+|\mathbf{I}\cap\mathbf{M}|]$, and~\eqref{eq:typeC-raw-newton-bound} follows.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
