<!-- thm-6: body_md -->
For every $s=|\mathbf{M}|$ with $r=|\mathbf{I}|\le s\le n$,
\begin{align}
&\varphi_{\mathbf{M}}\left(
\mathcal N\left(
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
\right)
\right)
\subset
\left[
-s^2+s-2r,
(N-s)(2N-s+2)-r+|\mathbf{I}\cap \mathbf{M}|
\right].
\label{eq:typeC-strong-newton-bound}
\end{align}
<!-- end thm-6 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Using the stability relation \eqref{eq:typeC-stability-s-ge-r}, the normalized definition \eqref{typeCnormalizeddefinition}, and then substituting $u_j=w_{m_j}$ into the normalization factor \eqref{typeCnormalizationfactor}, we obtain
\begin{align}
&\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
=(1-q)^{s-r}
\prod_{\substack{j\le k\\j,k\in\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}}
(1-qw_jw_k)
\frac{
WC_{N,s,r}(w_{m_1},\ldots,w_{m_s}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
}{
\displaystyle
\prod_{j,k=1}^{s}(1-qw_{m_j}^{-1}w_{m_k})
}.
 \notag
\end{align}
The degree intervals of the products appearing here are
\begin{align*}
(1-q)^{s-r}&:\ [0,0],\\
\prod_{j,k=1}^{s}(1-qw_{m_j}^{-1}w_{m_k})&:\ [0,0],\\
\prod_{\substack{j\le k\\j,k\in\{1,\dots,N\}\setminus \mathbf{M}}}(1-qw_jw_k)
&:\ \left[0,4\tbinom{N-s+1}{2} \right]
=\left[0,2(N-s)(N-s+1) \right].
\end{align*}
Thus the lower endpoint is unchanged, while the upper endpoint becomes
\[
s(N-s)-r+|\mathbf{I}\cap \mathbf{M}|+2(N-s)(N-s+1)
=(N-s)(2N-s+2)-r+|\mathbf{I}\cap \mathbf{M}|.
\]
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
Let $s=|\mathbf{M}|$ satisfy $r\le s\le n$. If $\mathbf{I}\not\subseteq \mathbf{M}$ as sets, then $|\mathbf{I}\cap \mathbf{M}|\le r-1$, and therefore
\begin{align}
&\varphi_{\mathbf{M}}\left(
\mathcal N\left(
\widetilde{WC}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
\right)
\right)
\subset[-s(s+1),(N-s)(2N-s+2)-1].
\label{eq:typeC-strict-smallness-sufficient-condition}
\end{align}
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Use $|\mathbf{I}\cap\mathbf{M}|\le r-1$ in
\eqref{eq:typeC-strong-newton-bound}. Since $s\ge r$, we also have
$-s^2+s-2r\ge -s(s+1)$, which gives the displayed weaker lower bound.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- prop-22: body_md -->
If $|\mathbf{M}|<|\mathbf{I}|=r$, then
\begin{equation}
\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})=0.
\label{eq:typeB-lower-cardinality-vanishing}
\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-22 -->
<!-- thm-7: body_md -->
For every $s=|\mathbf{M}|$ with $r=|\mathbf{I}|\le s\le n$,
\begin{align}
&\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
\notag\\
&=(1-q)^{s-r}
\prod_{j\in\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}(1-qw_j)
\prod_{\substack{j<k\\j,k\in\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}}(1-qw_jw_k)
\sum_{\substack{\mathbf{K}\subset\mathbf{M}\\|\mathbf{K}|=r}}
\frac{
W_{N,r}(w_{k_1},\ldots,w_{k_r}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
}{
\displaystyle
\prod_{a,b=1}^{r}(1-qw_{k_a}^{-1}w_{k_b})
}
\notag\\
&\quad\times
\frac{
\displaystyle
\prod_{a=1}^{r}(1-w_{k_a}^{-1})
\prod_{1\le a<b\le r}(1-w_{k_a}^{-1}w_{k_b}^{-1})
\prod_{a=1}^{r}\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}(1-w_{k_a}^{-1}w_{l_b}^{-1})
}{
\displaystyle
\prod_{a=1}^{r}\prod_{b=1}^{s-r}(1-w_{k_a}^{-1}w_{l_b})
}
\notag\\
&\quad\times
\prod_{1\le a<b\le s-r}(1-qw_{l_a}^{-1}w_{l_b}^{-1})
\prod_{a=1}^{s-r}\prod_{j\in\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}
(1-qw_{l_a}^{-1}w_j).
\label{eq:typeB-cross-sector-branching}
\end{align}
For $r=0$, set $W_{N,0}=1$, and interpret every empty product as $1$.
If $s=r$, only the term $\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{M}$ remains, and the formula agrees with
\eqref{relationwithAnormalizedtypeB}.
<!-- end thm-7 -->
<!-- prop-23: body_md -->
Let $s=|\mathbf{M}|$ and assume $r\le s\le n$. Then
\[
\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})\ne0
\]
only if
\begin{equation}
i_j\le m_{s-r+j}
\qquad(1\le j\le r).
\label{eq:typeB-generalized-support-componentwise}
\end{equation}
This condition is equivalent to $\lambda(\mathbf{I})\subseteq\lambda(\mathbf{M})$.
<!-- end prop-23 -->
<!-- prop-24: body_md -->
For every $\mathbf{M}=\{m_1<\cdots<m_s\}\subset\{1,\dots,N\}$ with $s\le n$,
\[
\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
\in
\mathbb{C}(q)[w_1^{\pm1},\ldots,w_N^{\pm1}].
\]
<!-- end prop-24 -->
<!-- prop-25: body_md -->
For every $s=|\mathbf{M}|$ with $r\le s\le n$, the Laurent polynomial
\[
\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
\]
is divisible by
\begin{equation}
\prod_{j\in\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}(1-qw_j)
\prod_{\substack{j<k\\j,k\in\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm c}}}(1-qw_jw_k).
\label{eq:typeB-complement-factor}
\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-25 -->
<!-- prop-26: body_md -->
For every $s=|\mathbf{M}|$ with $r\le s\le n$,
\begin{equation}
\varphi_{\mathbf{M}}\left(
\mathcal N\left(
WB_{N,s,r}(w_{m_1},\ldots,w_{m_s}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
\right)
\right)
\subset
[-s^2+s-r,\ s(N-s)-r+|\mathbf{I}\cap\mathbf{M}|].
\label{eq:typeB-raw-newton-bound}
\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-26 -->
<!-- thm-8: body_md -->
For every $s=|\mathbf{M}|$ with $r=|\mathbf{I}|\le s\le n$,
\begin{align}
&\varphi_{\mathbf{M}}\left(
\mathcal N\left(
\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
\right)
\right)
\subset
\left[
-s^2+s-r,
(N-s)(2N-s)-r+|\mathbf{I}\cap\mathbf{M}|
\right].
\label{eq:typeB-strong-newton-bound}
\end{align}
<!-- end thm-8 -->
<!-- corollary-6: body_md -->
Let $s=|\mathbf{M}|$ satisfy $r\le s\le n$. If $\mathbf{I}\not\subseteq\mathbf{M}$ as sets, then $|\mathbf{I}\cap\mathbf{M}|\le r-1$, and therefore
\begin{align}
&\varphi_{\mathbf{M}}\left(
\mathcal N\left(
\widetilde{WB}_{N,n,r}(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{M}}|\mathbf{w}|\mathbf{I})
\right)
\right)
\subset[-s^2,(N-s)(2N-s)-1].
\label{eq:typeB-strict-smallness-sufficient-condition}
\end{align}
<!-- end corollary-6 -->
<!-- thm-9: body_md -->
\label{thm:BC-equal-cardinality-orthogonality}
For $X\in\{B,C\}$ and any $r$-element subsets
$\mathbf J,\mathbf K\subset[N]$,
\begin{align}
&\sum_{\substack{\mathbf M\subset[N]\\|\mathbf M|=r}}
\frac{
q^{p(\mathbf K)}P_{\mathbf M}(\mathbf w)
}{
R_{\mathbf M}(\mathbf w)
Q_{\mathbf M}(\mathbf w,q)
D^X_r(\mathbf M;\mathbf w,q)
D^X_r(\mathbf M;\mathbf w^{-1},q^{-1})
}
\notag\\
&\qquad\times
\widetilde{WX}_{N,n,r}
\bigl((\mathbf w^{\rho})_{\rho(\mathbf M)}
\,|\mathbf w^{\rho}\,|\rho(\mathbf J)\bigr)
\left.
\widetilde{WX}_{N,n,r}
\bigl((\mathbf w^{-1})_{\mathbf M}
\,|\mathbf w^{-1}\,|\mathbf K\bigr)
\right|_{q\mapsto q^{-1}}
=\delta_{\mathbf J,\mathbf K}.
\label{eq:BC-equal-cardinality-orthogonality}
\end{align}
<!-- end thm-9 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
This is a direct rewriting of the type $A$ orthogonality relation
\eqref{eq:RTV-Gr-orthogonality}.  Setting $\mathbf z=\mathbf w$ and $h=q$
in that relation gives
\begin{align*}
\delta_{\mathbf J,\mathbf K}
={}&
\sum_{\substack{\mathbf M\subset[N]\\ |\mathbf M|=r}}
q^{p(\mathbf K)}
\frac{
P_{\mathbf M}(\mathbf w)
}{
R_{\mathbf M}(\mathbf w)Q_{\mathbf M}(\mathbf w,q)
}
\\
&\qquad\times
\widetilde W^{\mathrm{RTV}}_{\mathrm{id},\mathcal J}
(w_{m_1},\ldots,w_{m_r},\mathbf w,q)
\,
\widetilde W^{\mathrm{RTV}}_{\sigma_0,\mathcal K}
(w_{m_1}^{-1},\ldots,w_{m_r}^{-1},
 \mathbf w^{-1},q^{-1}),
\end{align*}
where
$
\mathcal J=(\mathbf J,\mathbf J^{\mathrm c}), \
\mathcal K=(\mathbf K,\mathbf K^{\mathrm c}).$
By \eqref{eq:PhiX-def} and
\eqref{eq:RTV-id-via-reversal},
\begin{equation}
\widetilde W^{\mathrm{RTV}}_{\mathrm{id},\mathcal J}
(w_{m_1},\ldots,w_{m_r},\mathbf w,q)
=
\Phi_{\rho(\mathbf M),\rho(\mathbf J)}
(\mathbf w^{\rho},q).
\label{eq:PhiX-op-is-id}
\end{equation}
Using \eqref{eq:PhiX-is-RTV} with
$(\mathbf M,\mathbf I,\mathbf w)$ replaced by
$(\rho(\mathbf M),\rho(\mathbf J),\mathbf w^{\rho})$, and then
\eqref{eq:local-D-reversal}, we obtain
\[
\widetilde W^{\mathrm{RTV}}_{\mathrm{id},\mathcal J}
(w_{m_1},\ldots,w_{m_r},\mathbf w,q)
=
\frac{
\widetilde{WX}_{N,n,r}
\bigl(
(\mathbf w^{\rho})_{\rho(\mathbf M)}
\,|\mathbf w^{\rho}\,|\rho(\mathbf J)
\bigr)
}{
D^X_r(\mathbf M;\mathbf w,q)
}.
\]
Similarly, \eqref{eq:PhiX-def} and \eqref{eq:PhiX-is-RTV}, after the
substitution $(\mathbf w,q)\mapsto(\mathbf w^{-1},q^{-1})$, give
\[
\widetilde W^{\mathrm{RTV}}_{\sigma_0,\mathcal K}
(w_{m_1}^{-1},\ldots,w_{m_r}^{-1},
 \mathbf w^{-1},q^{-1})
=
\frac{
\left.
\widetilde{WX}_{N,n,r}
\bigl(
(\mathbf w^{-1})_{\mathbf M}
\,|\mathbf w^{-1}\,|\mathbf K
\bigr)
\right|_{q\mapsto q^{-1}}
}{
D^X_r(\mathbf M;\mathbf w^{-1},q^{-1})
}.
\]
Substituting these two identities into
\eqref{eq:RTV-Gr-orthogonality} gives
\eqref{eq:BC-equal-cardinality-orthogonality}.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- corollary-7: body_md -->
\label{cor:Phi-inverse}
The matrix $\Phi^{(r)}(\mathbf w,q)$ is invertible.  Its inverse is given by
\begin{align}
\bigl(\Phi^{(r)}(\mathbf w,q)^{-1}\bigr)_{\mathbf I,\mathbf K}
={}&
q^{-p(\mathbf I)}
\frac{
P_{\mathbf K}(\mathbf w^{-1})
}{
R_{\mathbf K}(\mathbf w^{-1})
Q_{\mathbf K}(\mathbf w^{-1},q^{-1})
}
\Phi_{\rho(\mathbf K),\rho(\mathbf I)}
\bigl((\mathbf w^{-1})^{\rho},q^{-1}\bigr).
\label{eq:Phi-inverse}
\end{align}
<!-- end corollary-7 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:BC-equal-cardinality-orthogonality} after the
substitution $(\mathbf w,q)\mapsto(\mathbf w^{-1},q^{-1})$, and use
\eqref{eq:PhiX-is-RTV} to replace the two normalized type $B/C$ weight
functions by the corresponding $\Phi$-factors.  After cancelling the
$D^X_r$-factors, the resulting orthogonality identity is precisely the
matrix-inverse relation for $\Phi^{(r)}(\mathbf w,q)$, with the inverse
entries given by the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:Phi-inverse}.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- head-2: body_md -->
Take $N=2$, $n=r=1$, and $X=C$, and let
$
\mathbf J=\{2\}, \ \mathbf K=\{1\}.
$
Then
$
\rho(\mathbf J)=\{1\},\ p(\mathbf K)=1.
$
The sum in Theorem~\ref{thm:BC-equal-cardinality-orthogonality}
has two terms, corresponding to
$\mathbf M=\{1\}$ and $\mathbf M=\{2\}$.
From \eqref{eq:RTV-Gr-P}--\eqref{eq:RTV-Gr-Q},
\begin{align*}
P_{\{1\}}(\mathbf w)&=-\frac{w_2}{w_1},&
R_{\{1\}}(\mathbf w)&=1-\frac{w_2}{w_1},&
Q_{\{1\}}(\mathbf w,q)&=1-q\frac{w_2}{w_1},\\
P_{\{2\}}(\mathbf w)&=-\frac{w_1}{w_2},&
R_{\{2\}}(\mathbf w)&=1-\frac{w_1}{w_2},&
Q_{\{2\}}(\mathbf w,q)&=1-q\frac{w_1}{w_2}.
\end{align*}
Moreover,
\begin{align*}
D^C_1(\{1\};\mathbf w,q)&=(1-w_1^{-2})(1-qw_2^2),\\
D^C_1(\{2\};\mathbf w,q)&=(1-w_2^{-2})(1-qw_1^2),\\
D^C_1(\{1\};\mathbf w^{-1},q^{-1})
&=(1-w_1^2)(1-q^{-1}w_2^{-2}),\\
D^C_1(\{2\};\mathbf w^{-1},q^{-1})
&=(1-w_2^2)(1-q^{-1}w_1^{-2}).
\end{align*}
Using \eqref{eq:PhiX-is-RTV}, the four values appearing in the two summands are
\begin{align}
\widetilde{WC}_{2,1,1}
\bigl(w_1\,|w_2,w_1\,|\{1\}\bigr)
&=D^C_1(\{1\};\mathbf w,q)(1-q),
 \notag\\
\widetilde{WC}_{2,1,1}
\bigl(w_2\,|w_2,w_1\,|\{1\}\bigr)
&=D^C_1(\{2\};\mathbf w,q)
\left(1-q\frac{w_1}{w_2}\right),
 \notag\\
\left.\widetilde{WC}_{2,1,1}
\bigl(w_1^{-1}\,|w_1^{-1},w_2^{-1}\,|\{1\}\bigr)
\right|_{q\mapsto q^{-1}}
&=D^C_1(\{1\};\mathbf w^{-1},q^{-1})
\left(1-q^{-1}\frac{w_1}{w_2}\right),
 \notag\\
\left.\widetilde{WC}_{2,1,1}
\bigl(w_2^{-1}\,|w_1^{-1},w_2^{-1}\,|\{1\}\bigr)
\right|_{q\mapsto q^{-1}}
&=D^C_1(\{2\};\mathbf w^{-1},q^{-1})(1-q^{-1}).
 \notag
\end{align}
Substituting these expressions into
Theorem~\ref{thm:BC-equal-cardinality-orthogonality},
the factors $D^C_1$ cancel.  The contribution from
$\mathbf M=\{1\}$ is
\begin{align}
T_{\{1\}}
&=q\frac{-w_2/w_1}
{(1-w_2/w_1)(1-qw_2/w_1)}
(1-q)\left(1-q^{-1}\frac{w_1}{w_2}\right)=\frac{1-q}{1-w_2/w_1},
 \notag
\end{align}
whereas the contribution from $\mathbf M=\{2\}$ is
\begin{align}
T_{\{2\}}
&=q\frac{-w_1/w_2}
{(1-w_1/w_2)(1-qw_1/w_2)}
\left(1-q\frac{w_1}{w_2}\right)(1-q^{-1})=-\frac{1-q}{1-w_2/w_1}.
 \notag
\end{align}
Therefore
$
T_{\{1\}}+T_{\{2\}}=0
=\delta_{\mathbf J,\mathbf K},
$
as required.
<!-- end head-2 -->
<!-- thm-10: body_md -->
\label{thm:BC-all-cardinality-biorthogonality}
For $X\in\{B,C\}$ and arbitrary subsets $\mathbf M,\mathbf L\subset[N]$,
\begin{equation}
\sum_{\mathbf I\subset[N]}
q^{|\lambda(\mathbf I)|-|\lambda(\mathbf L)|}
\mathsf A^X_{\mathbf M,\mathbf I}(\mathbf w,q)
\mathsf A^X_{\mathbf L^{\mathrm c},\mathbf I^{\mathrm c}}
(\mathbf w,q^{-1})
=\delta_{\mathbf M,\mathbf L}.
\label{eq:BC-all-cardinality-biorthogonality-matrix}
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{align}
&\sum_{\mathbf I\subset[N]}
q^{|\lambda(\mathbf I)|-|\lambda(\mathbf L)|}
\frac{
\widetilde{WX}_{N,N,|\mathbf I|}
(\mathbf w_{\mathbf M}\,|\mathbf w\,|\mathbf I)
}{\Delta^X_{\mathbf M}(\mathbf w,q)}
\frac{
\left.
\widetilde{WX}_{N,N,N-|\mathbf I|}
(\mathbf w_{\mathbf L^{\mathrm c}}\,|\mathbf w\,|\mathbf I^{\mathrm c})
\right|_{q\mapsto q^{-1}}
}{
\Delta^X_{\mathbf L^{\mathrm c}}(\mathbf w,q^{-1})
}
=\delta_{\mathbf M,\mathbf L}.
\label{eq:BC-all-cardinality-biorthogonality}
\end{align}
<!-- end thm-10 -->
<!-- lem-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:allcard-Phihat-inverse}
For arbitrary subsets $\mathbf I,\mathbf K\subset[N]$,
\begin{equation}
\bigl(\widehat\Phi(\mathbf w,q)^{-1}\bigr)_{\mathbf I,\mathbf K}
=
q^{|\lambda(\mathbf I)|-|\lambda(\mathbf K)|}
\widehat\Phi_{\mathbf K^{\mathrm c},\mathbf I^{\mathrm c}}
(\mathbf w,q^{-1}).
\label{eq:allcard-Phihat-inverse-components}
\end{equation}
Both sides are zero unless $|\mathbf I|=|\mathbf K|$.
<!-- end lem-4 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
By definition, $\widehat\Phi(\mathbf w,q)$ is block diagonal with respect to
cardinality.  Hence its inverse is block diagonal as well, so the left-hand
side of \eqref{eq:allcard-Phihat-inverse-components} vanishes when
$|\mathbf I|\ne|\mathbf K|$.  On the right-hand side,
$\widehat\Phi_{\mathbf K^{\mathrm c},\mathbf I^{\mathrm c}}$ vanishes unless
$|\mathbf K^{\mathrm c}|=|\mathbf I^{\mathrm c}|$, which is equivalent to
$|\mathbf K|=|\mathbf I|$.  It remains to consider
$|\mathbf I|=|\mathbf K|=r$.

By \eqref{eq:allcard-Phihat-def},
\[
\widehat\Phi^{(r)}(\mathbf w,q)
=
\operatorname{diag}
\bigl(\Phi_{\mathbf A,\mathbf A}(\mathbf w,q)^{-1}\bigr)_{|\mathbf A|=r}
\Phi^{(r)}(\mathbf w,q),
\]
and therefore
\[
\bigl(\widehat\Phi(\mathbf w,q)^{-1}\bigr)_{\mathbf I,\mathbf K}
=
\bigl(\Phi^{(r)}(\mathbf w,q)^{-1}\bigr)_{\mathbf I,\mathbf K}
\Phi_{\mathbf K,\mathbf K}(\mathbf w,q).
\]
Together with Corollary~\ref{cor:Phi-inverse}, this gives
\begin{align}
\bigl(\widehat\Phi(\mathbf w,q)^{-1}\bigr)_{\mathbf I,\mathbf K}
={}&
q^{-p(\mathbf I)}
\frac{
P_{\mathbf K}(\mathbf w^{-1})
\Phi_{\mathbf K,\mathbf K}(\mathbf w,q)
}{
R_{\mathbf K}(\mathbf w^{-1})
Q_{\mathbf K}(\mathbf w^{-1},q^{-1})
}
\Phi_{\rho(\mathbf K),\rho(\mathbf I)}
\bigl((\mathbf w^{-1})^{\rho},q^{-1}\bigr).
\label{eq:allcard-Phihat-inverse-before-duality}
\end{align}

For a subspace $\mathcal U\subset\mathbb C^N$, put
$
\mathcal U^{\perp}
:=\{f\in(\mathbb C^N)^*:f|_{\mathcal U}=0\}.
$
Then $\mathcal U\mapsto\mathcal U^{\perp}$ identifies
$\operatorname{Gr}(r,\mathbb C^N)$ with
$\operatorname{Gr}(N-r,(\mathbb C^N)^*)$.
The coordinate fixed point indexed by $\mathbf M$ is sent to the one indexed
by $\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}$, and the torus weights on $(\mathbb C^N)^*$ are
$z_1^{-1},\ldots,z_N^{-1}$.  This identification lifts to the cotangent
bundles and, after inversion of the torus characters, induces the corresponding
equivariant $K$-theory isomorphism.  Hence the transformed fixed-point
restrictions below come from an equivariant $K$-class on
$T^*\operatorname{Gr}(N-r,(\mathbb C^N)^*)$.
Let $h$ be the parameter in Theorem~\ref{RTVtheorem}, and put
$
\mathcal M=(\mathbf M,\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}),
\
\mathcal M^{\vee}=(\mathbf M^{\mathrm c},\mathbf M).
$
We check conditions $\mathrm{(i)}$--$\mathrm{(iii)}$ of
Theorem~\ref{RTVtheorem} after the substitution
$\mathbf z\mapsto\mathbf z^{-1}$ and the replacement
$\mathcal M\mapsto\mathcal M^{\vee}$.
For condition $\mathrm{(i)}$,
\begin{align}
e_{\mathrm{id},\mathcal M,-}^{\mathrm{vert}}
(\mathbf z^{-1},h)
&=
\prod_{\substack{a\in\mathbf M,\ b\in\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}\\ b<a}}
\left(1-h\frac{z_a}{z_b}\right)
=
 e_{\sigma_0,\mathcal M^{\vee},-}^{\mathrm{vert}}
(\mathbf z,h).
 \notag
\end{align}
For condition $\mathrm{(ii)}$, put
$\mathcal I^{\vee}=(\mathbf I^{\mathrm c},\mathbf I)$.  At the diagonal fixed
point, \eqref{general-diagonal-factor} gives
\begin{align}
&P_{\mathrm{id},\mathcal I}(\mathbf z^{-1})
 e_{\mathrm{id},\mathcal I}(\mathbf z^{-1},h)
=
\prod_{\substack{a\in\mathbf I,\ b\in\mathbf I^{\mathrm c}\\ a<b}}
\left(1-\frac{z_a}{z_b}\right)
\prod_{\substack{a\in\mathbf I,\ b\in\mathbf I^{\mathrm c}\\ b<a}}
\left(1-h\frac{z_a}{z_b}\right)
\notag\\
&=
P_{\sigma_0,\mathcal I^{\vee}}(\mathbf z)
 e_{\sigma_0,\mathcal I^{\vee}}(\mathbf z,h).
 \notag
\end{align}

It remains to check the smallness condition $\mathrm{(iii)}$.  Suppose
$\mathcal M\ne\mathcal I$ and set
$
f_{\mathcal M}(\mathbf z)
:=
\widetilde W_{\mathrm{id},\mathcal I}^{\mathrm{RTV}}
(\mathbf z_{\mathbf M},\mathbf z,h).
$
By \eqref{eq:general-sigma-W} and \eqref{eq:general-Wtilde-def}, after the
fixed-point specialization $\mathbf t=\mathbf z_{\mathbf M}$ all the
$z$-variables occur through ratios $z_a/z_b$.  Hence every Laurent monomial
$
\mathbf z^{\nu}=z_1^{\nu_1}\cdots z_N^{\nu_N}
$
occurring in $f_{\mathcal M}(\mathbf z)$ satisfies
\begin{align}
\sum_{j=1}^N\nu_j=0. \label{sumtozero}
\end{align}
Under $\mathbf z\mapsto\mathbf z^{-1}$ the monomial $\mathbf z^\nu$ becomes
$\mathbf z^{-\nu}$, and
\begin{align}
\varphi_{\mathcal M^{\vee}}(-\nu)
&=-\sum_{j\in\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}}\nu_j
  -2\sum_{j\in\mathbf M}\nu_j
=\sum_{j\in\mathbf M}\nu_j
  +2\sum_{j\in\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}}\nu_j
=\varphi_{\mathcal M}(\nu),
 \notag
\end{align}
where the second equality uses \eqref{sumtozero}.  Moreover,
\[
\mathcal N\bigl(f_{\mathcal M}(\mathbf z^{-1})\bigr)
=-\mathcal N\bigl(f_{\mathcal M}(\mathbf z)\bigr).
\]
Therefore
\[
\varphi_{\mathcal M^{\vee}}
\bigl(\mathcal N(f_{\mathcal M}(\mathbf z^{-1}))\bigr)
=
\varphi_{\mathcal M}
\bigl(\mathcal N(f_{\mathcal M}(\mathbf z))\bigr)
\subset[0,r(N-r)-1].
\]
Since $\mathcal M\ne\mathcal I$ implies
$\mathcal M^{\vee}\ne\mathcal I^{\vee}$, and the smallness interval for
$\operatorname{Gr}(N-r,N)$ is again $[0,r(N-r)-1]$, condition
$\mathrm{(iii)}$ is preserved.

The uniqueness statement in Theorem~\ref{RTVtheorem} therefore gives, for
all $\mathbf M\subset[N]$ with $|\mathbf M|=r$,
\begin{equation}
\widetilde W_{\mathrm{id},\mathcal I}^{\mathrm{RTV}}
(\mathbf z_{\mathbf M}^{-1},\mathbf z^{-1},h)
=
\widetilde W_{\sigma_0,\mathcal I^{\vee}}^{\mathrm{RTV}}
(\mathbf z_{\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}},\mathbf z,h).
\label{eq:allcard-RTV-complement-duality}
\end{equation}
Taking $\mathbf M=\mathbf K$, $\mathbf z=\mathbf w$, and $h=q^{-1}$, and
using \eqref{eq:PhiX-def} and \eqref{eq:PhiX-op-is-id}, we obtain
\begin{equation}
\Phi_{\rho(\mathbf K),\rho(\mathbf I)}
\bigl((\mathbf w^{-1})^{\rho},q^{-1}\bigr)
=
\Phi_{\mathbf K^{\mathrm c},\mathbf I^{\mathrm c}}
(\mathbf w,q^{-1}).
\label{eq:allcard-Phi-complement-duality}
\end{equation}

By \eqref{eq:RTV-Gr-P}--\eqref{eq:RTV-Gr-Q},
Theorem~\ref{RTVtheorem} $\mathrm{(ii)}$, and
\eqref{general-diagonal-factor}, after simplification we obtain
\begin{equation}
\frac{
P_{\mathbf K}(\mathbf w^{-1})
\Phi_{\mathbf K,\mathbf K}(\mathbf w,q)
\Phi_{\mathbf K^{\mathrm c},\mathbf K^{\mathrm c}}(\mathbf w,q^{-1})
}{
R_{\mathbf K}(\mathbf w^{-1})
Q_{\mathbf K}(\mathbf w^{-1},q^{-1})
}
=q^{p(\mathbf K)}.
\label{eq:allcard-scalar-complement}
\end{equation}
Substituting \eqref{eq:allcard-Phi-complement-duality} and
\eqref{eq:allcard-scalar-complement} into
\eqref{eq:allcard-Phihat-inverse-before-duality} yields
\begin{align}
\bigl(\widehat\Phi(\mathbf w,q)^{-1}\bigr)_{\mathbf I,\mathbf K}
&=
q^{-p(\mathbf I)+p(\mathbf K)}
\frac{
\Phi_{\mathbf K^{\mathrm c},\mathbf I^{\mathrm c}}(\mathbf w,q^{-1})
}{
\Phi_{\mathbf K^{\mathrm c},\mathbf K^{\mathrm c}}(\mathbf w,q^{-1})
}
=
q^{-p(\mathbf I)+p(\mathbf K)}
\widehat\Phi_{\mathbf K^{\mathrm c},\mathbf I^{\mathrm c}}
(\mathbf w,q^{-1}).
\label{eq:allcard-Psihat-before-lambda}
\end{align}
If $\mathbf A=\{a_1<\cdots<a_r\}$, then by \eqref{eq:RTV-Gr-p} and
$|\lambda(\mathbf A)|=\sum_{j=1}^r a_j$,
\begin{align}
p(\mathbf A)+|\lambda(\mathbf A)|
&=
\sum_{j=1}^r(N-r+j-a_j)+\sum_{j=1}^r a_j
=
r(N-r)+\frac{r(r+1)}2
=
rN-\binom r2.
 \notag
\end{align}
Since the right-hand side depends only on $r$,
\[
p(\mathbf K)-p(\mathbf I)
=
|\lambda(\mathbf I)|-|\lambda(\mathbf K)|.
\]
Substituting this into \eqref{eq:allcard-Psihat-before-lambda} proves
\eqref{eq:allcard-Phihat-inverse-components}.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- lem-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:allcard-Bhat-product}
Let $\mathbf K\subset\mathbf M$ and put
$\Omega=\mathbf M\setminus\mathbf K$.  Then
\begin{align}
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf M,\mathbf K}(\mathbf w,q)
={}&
(1-q)^{|\Omega|}
\prod_{t\in\Omega}\frac{1}{1-w_t^{-2}}
\prod_{\substack{a<b\\a,b\in\Omega}}
\frac{1-qw_a^{-1}w_b^{-1}}{1-w_a^{-1}w_b^{-1}}
\notag\\
&\times
\prod_{\substack{k\in\mathbf K,\ t\in\Omega\\k<t}}
\frac{1-qw_k^{-1}w_t}{1-w_k^{-1}w_t}
\prod_{\substack{t\in\Omega,\ j\in\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}\\j<t}}
\frac{1-qw_t^{-1}w_j}{1-w_t^{-1}w_j},
\label{eq:allcard-Bhat-product-C}
\end{align}
and
\begin{align}
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^B_{\mathbf M,\mathbf K}(\mathbf w,q)
={}&
(1-q)^{|\Omega|}
\prod_{t\in\Omega}\frac{1}{1-w_t^{-1}}
\prod_{\substack{a<b\\a,b\in\Omega}}
\frac{1-qw_a^{-1}w_b^{-1}}{1-w_a^{-1}w_b^{-1}}
\notag\\
&\times
\prod_{\substack{k\in\mathbf K,\ t\in\Omega\\k<t}}
\frac{1-qw_k^{-1}w_t}{1-w_k^{-1}w_t}
\prod_{\substack{t\in\Omega,\ j\in\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}\\j<t}}
\frac{1-qw_t^{-1}w_j}{1-w_t^{-1}w_j}.
\label{eq:allcard-Bhat-product-B}
\end{align}
<!-- end lem-5 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
We give the calculation for type $C$.  By
\eqref{eq:allcard-Bhat-def} and \eqref{eq:allcard-Delta-factor},
\[
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf M,\mathbf K}(\mathbf w,q)
=
\frac{
\operatorname{Br}^C_{\mathbf M,\mathbf K}(\mathbf w,q)
\Phi_{\mathbf K,\mathbf K}(\mathbf w,q)
}{D^C_{|\mathbf M|}(\mathbf M;\mathbf w,q)
\Phi_{\mathbf M,\mathbf M}(\mathbf w,q)
}
.
\]
Put $\Omega=\mathbf M\setminus\mathbf K$.  The four factors on the
right-hand side are as follows.  First,
\begin{align*}
\operatorname{Br}^C_{\mathbf M,\mathbf K}(\mathbf w,q)
={}&
(1-q)^{|\Omega|}
\prod_{\substack{a\le b\\a,b\in\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}}}
(1-qw_aw_b)
\prod_{\substack{a\le b\\a,b\in\mathbf K}}
(1-w_a^{-1}w_b^{-1})
\\
&\times
\prod_{\substack{k\in\mathbf K\\t\in\Omega}}
\frac{1-w_k^{-1}w_t^{-1}}{1-w_k^{-1}w_t}
\prod_{\substack{a<b\\a,b\in\Omega}}
(1-qw_a^{-1}w_b^{-1})
\prod_{\substack{t\in\Omega\\j\in\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}}}
(1-qw_t^{-1}w_j),
\end{align*}
and
\[
D^C_{|\mathbf M|}(\mathbf M;\mathbf w,q)
=
\prod_{\substack{a\le b\\a,b\in\mathbf M}}
(1-w_a^{-1}w_b^{-1})
\prod_{\substack{a\le b\\a,b\in\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}}}
(1-qw_aw_b).
\]
Next, if
$\mathbf K=\{k_1<\cdots<k_r\}$ and
$\mathbf M=\{m_1<\cdots<m_s\}$, then by \eqref{eq:PhiX-def},
Theorem~\ref{RTVtheorem} $\mathrm{(ii)}$, and
\eqref{general-diagonal-factor},
\begin{align*}
\Phi_{\mathbf K,\mathbf K}(\mathbf w,q)
&=
\widetilde W^{\mathrm{RTV}}_{\sigma_0,\mathcal K}
(w_{k_1},\ldots,w_{k_r},\mathbf w,q)\\
&=
\prod_{\substack{k\in\mathbf K,\ a\in\mathbf K^{\mathrm c}\\a<k}}
(1-w_k^{-1}w_a)
\prod_{\substack{k\in\mathbf K,\ a\in\mathbf K^{\mathrm c}\\k<a}}
(1-qw_k^{-1}w_a),
\\[1mm]
\Phi_{\mathbf M,\mathbf M}(\mathbf w,q)
&=
\widetilde W^{\mathrm{RTV}}_{\sigma_0,\mathcal M}
(w_{m_1},\ldots,w_{m_s},\mathbf w,q)\\
&=
\prod_{\substack{m\in\mathbf M,\ j\in\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}\\j<m}}
(1-w_m^{-1}w_j)
\prod_{\substack{m\in\mathbf M,\ j\in\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}\\m<j}}
(1-qw_m^{-1}w_j),
\end{align*}
where
$\mathcal K=(\mathbf K,\mathbf K^{\mathrm c})$ and
$\mathcal M=(\mathbf M,\mathbf M^{\mathrm c})$.
Substituting these four expressions and simplifying, using
\[
\mathbf M=\mathbf K\sqcup\Omega,
\qquad
\mathbf K^{\mathrm c}=\Omega\sqcup\mathbf M^{\mathrm c},
\]
gives
\eqref{eq:allcard-Bhat-product-C}.  The type $B$ formula is obtained
by the same simplification from \eqref{eq:allcard-Br-B} and
\eqref{eq:local-DB-orth}.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- lem-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:allcard-universal-cancellation}
For $m\ge1$ and generic variables $x_1,\ldots,x_m$,
\begin{equation}
\sum_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^m}
(-1)^{m-|\epsilon|}
\prod_{1\le i<j\le m}
H_{\epsilon_i,\epsilon_j}(x_i,x_j)=0,
\label{eq:allcard-universal-cancellation}
\end{equation}
where $|\epsilon|=\epsilon_1+\cdots+\epsilon_m$.
<!-- end lem-6 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Denote the left-hand side of
\eqref{eq:allcard-universal-cancellation} by
$F_m(x_1,\ldots,x_m)$ and regard it as a rational function of $x_m$.
The only possible finite poles are $x_m=x_i$ with $i<m$.  The residues of
the two off-diagonal factors are
\begin{align}
\operatorname*{Res}_{y=x}H_{01}(x,y)
&=x(1-q)(x^2-1),\ \ \
\operatorname*{Res}_{y=x}H_{10}(x,y)
=-x(1-q)(x^2-1).
 \notag
\end{align}
Fix $\epsilon_j$ for $j\ne i,m$.  The two terms with
$(\epsilon_i,\epsilon_m)=(0,1)$ and $(1,0)$ have the same alternating sign,
and at $x_m=x_i$ all other pair factors agree after interchanging
$\epsilon_i$ and $\epsilon_m$, using
$H_{a,b}(x,y)=H_{b,a}(y,x)$.  Their residues therefore cancel.  Hence
$F_m$ is a polynomial in $x_m$.

To determine the degree in $x_m$, keep $x_1,\ldots,x_{m-1}$ fixed and
set $y=x_m$.  From the definitions of the four functions $H_{a,b}$,
\begin{equation*}
H_{a,b}(x,y)=q^a x y+O(1)
\qquad (y\to\infty),
\qquad a,b\in\{0,1\}.
\end{equation*}
In particular, the coefficient of the leading term is independent of the
second index $b$.  Fix $\epsilon_1,\ldots,\epsilon_{m-1}$.  The factors in
the corresponding summand of $F_m$ that contain $x_m$ satisfy
\begin{align}
\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}
H_{\epsilon_i,\epsilon_m}(x_i,x_m)
={}&
q^{\epsilon_1+\cdots+\epsilon_{m-1}}
\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}x_i\right)x_m^{m-1}
+O(x_m^{m-2}).
 \notag
\end{align}
The leading term on the right-hand side is the same for
$\epsilon_m=0$ and $\epsilon_m=1$, whereas the alternating signs of these
two terms in $F_m$ are opposite.  Hence their coefficients of
$x_m^{m-1}$ cancel.  Therefore
\begin{equation}
\deg_{x_m}F_m\le m-2.
\label{eq:allcard-degree}
\end{equation}

We now prove that $F_m$ has $m-1$ roots.  The case $m=1$ is immediate.
For $m=2$, \eqref{eq:allcard-degree} shows that $F_2$ is constant, while
\[
F_2(x_1,x_1^{-1})
=H_{00}(x_1,x_1^{-1})+H_{11}(x_1,x_1^{-1})=0.
\]
Hence $F_2=0$.  Assume $m\ge3$ and that the identity is known for $m-2$.
Fix $k<m$ and
specialize $x_m=x_k^{-1}$.  Since
$
H_{01}(x_k,x_k^{-1})=H_{10}(x_k,x_k^{-1})=0,
$
only the terms with
$
\epsilon_k=\epsilon_m=:s,
\ s\in\{0,1\},
$
survive.  Let $j\ne k,m$, put $u=x_j$, and write
$\eta=\epsilon_j$.  Using
$H_{ab}(x,y)=H_{ba}(y,x)$ when necessary, the product of the two factors
involving $x_j$ and the variables $x_k,x_m$ can be written as
$H_{\eta,s}(u,x_k)H_{\eta,s}(u,x_k^{-1})$.  For
$\eta\in\{0,1\}$ one has
\begin{align}
H_{\eta,0}(u,x)H_{\eta,0}(u,x^{-1})
&=\frac{(ux-q)(u-qx)}{x}, \
H_{\eta,1}(u,x)H_{\eta,1}(u,x^{-1})
=\frac{(qu-x)(qux-1)}{x}.
\label{eq:allcard-fusion}
\end{align}
For fixed $s$, the right-hand side is independent of the remaining bit
$\eta=\epsilon_j$.  Hence all factors involving $k$ or $m$ are independent
of $\epsilon_j$ for $j\ne k,m$ and may be taken outside the alternating sum
over the remaining bits.  The remaining product and alternating signs are
exactly those defining $F_{m-2}$ in the variables
$x_1,\ldots,\widehat{x_k},\ldots,x_{m-1}$.  By the induction hypothesis,
this sum vanishes for each $s$.  Therefore
\begin{equation*}
F_m\big|_{x_m=x_k^{-1}}=0
\qquad(k=1,\ldots,m-1).
\end{equation*}
The polynomial $F_m$ therefore has degree at most $m-2$ and the $m-1$
distinct roots $x_1^{-1},\ldots,x_{m-1}^{-1}$.  Hence $F_m=0$
identically.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- prop-27: body_md -->
\label{prop:allcard-Bhat-inverse}
For $X\in\{B,C\}$,
\begin{equation}
\bigl(\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^X(\mathbf w,q)^{-1}\bigr)_{\mathbf I,\mathbf M}
=
q^{|\lambda(\mathbf I)|-|\lambda(\mathbf M)|}
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^X_{\mathbf M^{\mathrm c},\mathbf I^{\mathrm c}}
(\mathbf w,q^{-1}).
\label{eq:allcard-Bhat-inverse}
\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-27 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
We prove the type $C$ case. The type $B$ case can be proved in the same way.
It is enough to multiply
$\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C(\mathbf w,q)$ by the matrix on the right-hand
side of \eqref{eq:allcard-Bhat-inverse} for $X=C$ and show that the product
is the identity.  Its $(\mathbf M,\mathbf L)$ entry is
\begin{equation}
\sum_{\mathbf K\subset[N]}
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf M,\mathbf K}(\mathbf w,q)
q^{|\lambda(\mathbf K)|-|\lambda(\mathbf L)|}
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf L^{\mathrm c},\mathbf K^{\mathrm c}}
(\mathbf w,q^{-1}).
\label{eq:allcard-boundary-product}
\end{equation}
By triangularity, a summand can be nonzero only when
$\mathbf L\subset\mathbf K\subset\mathbf M$.  If $\mathbf M=\mathbf L$,
only $\mathbf K=\mathbf M$ occurs, and the value is $1$ because the
diagonal entries of $\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C$ are $1$.

Assume now that $\mathbf L\subsetneq\mathbf M$ and write
\[
\mathcal D:=\mathbf M\setminus\mathbf L
=\{d_1<\cdots<d_m\}.
\]
Every intermediate subset $\mathbf K$ with
$\mathbf L\subset\mathbf K\subset\mathbf M$ is uniquely of the form
\[
\mathbf K=\mathbf K(\mathbf U):=\mathbf L\cup\mathbf U,
\qquad
\mathbf U\subset\mathcal D.
\]
For $\mathbf U\subset\mathcal D$, let
$\epsilon=(\epsilon_1,\ldots,\epsilon_m)\in\{0,1\}^m$ be defined by
\[
\epsilon_i=
\begin{cases}
1,& d_i\in\mathbf U,\\
0,& d_i\notin\mathbf U.
\end{cases}
\]
Since $|\lambda(\mathbf A)|=\sum_{a\in\mathbf A}a$, we have
\begin{equation*}
|\lambda(\mathbf K(\mathbf U))|-|\lambda(\mathbf L)|
=
\sum_{d\in\mathbf U}d
=
\sum_{i=1}^m d_i\epsilon_i.
\end{equation*}
Thus, if we set
\begin{equation}
S(\mathbf U)
:=
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf M,\mathbf K(\mathbf U)}(\mathbf w,q)
q^{\sum_{d\in\mathbf U}d}
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf L^{\mathrm c},
\mathbf K(\mathbf U)^{\mathrm c}}(\mathbf w,q^{-1}),
\label{eq:allcard-SU-def}
\end{equation}
then \eqref{eq:allcard-boundary-product} is simply
$\displaystyle
\sum_{\mathbf U\subset\mathcal D}S(\mathbf U).
$

Before treating the general case, it is useful to see explicitly what
happens when $|\mathcal D|=1$.  Write
$
\mathcal D=\{d\},
\
\mathbf M=\mathbf L\sqcup\{d\}.
$
There are only two subsets of $\mathcal D$, namely $\emptyset$ and
$\{d\}$.  If $\mathbf U=\emptyset$, then
$\mathbf K(\mathbf U)=\mathbf L$, and hence
\begin{align*}
S(\emptyset)
&=
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf M,\mathbf L}(\mathbf w,q)
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf L^{\mathrm c},\mathbf L^{\mathrm c}}
(\mathbf w,q^{-1})=
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf M,\mathbf L}(\mathbf w,q),
\end{align*}
since the diagonal entries of $\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C$ are $1$.
If $\mathbf U=\{d\}$, then $\mathbf K(\mathbf U)=\mathbf M$, so
\begin{align*}
S(\{d\})
&=
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf M,\mathbf M}(\mathbf w,q)
q^d
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf L^{\mathrm c},\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}}
(\mathbf w,q^{-1})=
q^d
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf L^{\mathrm c},\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}}
(\mathbf w,q^{-1}).
\end{align*}
Using \eqref{eq:allcard-Bhat-product-C}, these two nontrivial matrix entries
are
\begin{align*}
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf M,\mathbf L}(\mathbf w,q)
={}&
\frac{1-q}{1-w_d^{-2}}
\prod_{\substack{l\in\mathbf L\\ l<d}}
\frac{1-qw_l^{-1}w_d}{1-w_l^{-1}w_d}
\prod_{\substack{a\in\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}\\ a<d}}
\frac{1-qw_d^{-1}w_a}{1-w_d^{-1}w_a},\\
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf L^{\mathrm c},\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}}
(\mathbf w,q^{-1})
={}&
\frac{1-q^{-1}}{1-w_d^{-2}}
\prod_{\substack{a\in\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}\\ a<d}}
\frac{1-q^{-1}w_a^{-1}w_d}{1-w_a^{-1}w_d}
\prod_{\substack{l\in\mathbf L\\ l<d}}
\frac{1-q^{-1}w_d^{-1}w_l}{1-w_d^{-1}w_l}.
\end{align*}
For every index smaller than $d$,
\begin{align*}
\frac{1-q^{-1}w_a^{-1}w_d}{1-w_a^{-1}w_d}
&=
q^{-1}
\frac{1-qw_d^{-1}w_a}{1-w_d^{-1}w_a},\ \ \
\frac{1-q^{-1}w_d^{-1}w_l}{1-w_d^{-1}w_l}
=
q^{-1}
\frac{1-qw_l^{-1}w_d}{1-w_l^{-1}w_d}.
\end{align*}
Moreover,
$\displaystyle
\frac{1-q^{-1}}{1-q}=-q^{-1}.
$
Since $\mathbf M=\mathbf L\sqcup\{d\}$, every index smaller
than $d$ belongs to exactly one of $\mathbf L$ and
$\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}$.
Thus the two products contribute $d-1$ factors of $q^{-1}$
in total. Therefore
\begin{equation*}
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf L^{\mathrm c},\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}}
(\mathbf w,q^{-1})
=
-q^{-d}
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf M,\mathbf L}(\mathbf w,q).
\end{equation*}
Consequently,
\[
S(\emptyset)+S(\{d\})
=
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf M,\mathbf L}(\mathbf w,q)
+q^d
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf L^{\mathrm c},\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}}
(\mathbf w,q^{-1})
=0.
\]

We now prove the general case. Fix $\mathbf U\subseteq\mathcal D$, where
\[
\mathcal D=\mathbf M\setminus\mathbf L=\{d_1<\cdots<d_m\},
\qquad \mathbf K(\mathbf U)=\mathbf L\sqcup\mathbf U.
\]
Put $x_j=w_{d_j}$, and let $\epsilon_j=1$ if $d_j\in\mathbf U$, and
$\epsilon_j=0$ otherwise. Recall that
\[
S(\mathbf U)=
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf M,\mathbf K(\mathbf U)}(\mathbf w,q)
q^{\sum_jd_j\epsilon_j}
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf L^{\mathrm c},\mathbf K(\mathbf U)^{\mathrm c}}
(\mathbf w,q^{-1}).
\]
We first expand both branching coefficients, organize their factors, and
then compare the resulting expression with $S(\emptyset)$.
All quotients below are taken in $\mathbb C(q,w_1,\ldots,w_N)$.

Since $\mathbf M\setminus\mathbf K(\mathbf U)=\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U$,
applying \eqref{eq:allcard-Bhat-product-C} to the first coefficient gives
\begin{align}
&\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf M,\mathbf K(\mathbf U)}(\mathbf w,q)
\notag\\
&=(1-q)^{m-|\mathbf U|}
\prod_{t\in\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U}\frac{1}{1-w_t^{-2}}
\prod_{\substack{a<b\\a,b\in\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U}}
\frac{1-qw_a^{-1}w_b^{-1}}{1-w_a^{-1}w_b^{-1}}
\notag\\
&\quad\times
\prod_{\substack{k\in\mathbf L\sqcup\mathbf U,\ t\in\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U\\k<t}}
\frac{1-qw_k^{-1}w_t}{1-w_k^{-1}w_t}
\prod_{\substack{t\in\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U,\ b\in\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}\\b<t}}
\frac{1-qw_t^{-1}w_b}{1-w_t^{-1}w_b}.
\label{eq:allcard-SU-first-expanded}
\end{align}
For the second coefficient, note that
\[
[N]=\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}\sqcup\mathbf L\sqcup\mathcal D,
\qquad
\mathbf K(\mathbf U)=\mathbf L\sqcup\mathbf U,
\]
where $\mathbf U\subset\mathcal D$. Taking complements in $[N]$ and using
$A^{\mathrm c}\setminus B^{\mathrm c}=B\setminus A$ gives
\begin{gather*}
\mathbf L^{\mathrm c}\setminus\mathbf K(\mathbf U)^{\mathrm c}
=\mathbf K(\mathbf U)\setminus\mathbf L=\mathbf U,
\qquad
\mathbf K(\mathbf U)^{\mathrm c}
=\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}\sqcup(\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U),
\qquad
(\mathbf L^{\mathrm c})^{\mathrm c}=\mathbf L.
\end{gather*}
Thus \eqref{eq:allcard-Bhat-product-C}, with $q$ replaced by $q^{-1}$, gives
\begin{align}
&\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^C_{\mathbf L^{\mathrm c},\mathbf K(\mathbf U)^{\mathrm c}}
(\mathbf w,q^{-1})\notag\\
&=(1-q^{-1})^{|\mathbf U|}
\prod_{t\in\mathbf U}\frac{1}{1-w_t^{-2}}
\prod_{\substack{a<b\\a,b\in\mathbf U}}
\frac{1-q^{-1}w_a^{-1}w_b^{-1}}{1-w_a^{-1}w_b^{-1}}
\notag\\
&\quad\times
\prod_{\substack{k\in\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}\sqcup(\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U),\ t\in\mathbf U\\k<t}}
\frac{1-q^{-1}w_k^{-1}w_t}{1-w_k^{-1}w_t}
\prod_{\substack{t\in\mathbf U,\ b\in\mathbf L\\b<t}}
\frac{1-q^{-1}w_t^{-1}w_b}{1-w_t^{-1}w_b}.
\label{eq:allcard-SU-second-expanded}
\end{align}
In these formulas, the index in the last product is denoted by $b$ to
avoid confusion with the index $j$ in $d_j$.

The prefactors and the single-index products in
\eqref{eq:allcard-SU-first-expanded} and \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-second-expanded},
together with the explicit power of $q$ in $S(\mathbf U)$, multiply to
\[
q^{\sum_jd_j\epsilon_j}
(1-q)^{m-|\mathbf U|}(1-q^{-1})^{|\mathbf U|}
\prod_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{1-x_j^{-2}}.
\]
Here the two single-index products combine into the product over
$\mathcal D$, because $\mathcal D=(\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U)\sqcup\mathbf U$.

We now organize the remaining two-index factors in
\eqref{eq:allcard-SU-first-expanded} and \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-second-expanded}.
First fix $d_j\in\mathcal D$ and collect the factors involving $d_j$ and
an index in $\mathbf L$ or $\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}$.
These are precisely the indices not in $\mathcal D$, since
$[N]=\mathbf L\sqcup\mathcal D\sqcup\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}$.

Suppose that $\epsilon_j=0$, so that $d_j\in\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U$.
In the first mixed product in \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-first-expanded}, set
$t=d_j$ and restrict $k$ to $\mathbf L$.
In the last product in \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-first-expanded}, set $t=d_j$
and retain all $b\in\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}$ satisfying $b<d_j$.
These factors give
\begin{equation}
E_j^{(0)}:=
\prod_{\substack{k\in\mathbf L\\k<d_j}}
\frac{1-qw_k^{-1}x_j}{1-w_k^{-1}x_j}
\prod_{\substack{b\in\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}\\b<d_j}}
\frac{1-qx_j^{-1}w_b}{1-x_j^{-1}w_b}.
\label{eq:allcard-E0-def}
\end{equation}
The remaining choices $k\in\mathbf U$ in the first mixed product
(recall that $\mathbf K(\mathbf U)=\mathbf L\sqcup\mathbf U$) involve
two indices in $\mathcal D$ and will be considered below.
There is no factor involving $d_j$ and an index in $\mathbf L$ or
$\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}$ in \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-second-expanded}:
every two-index factor in \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-second-expanded} contains
an index in $\mathbf U$, whereas neither member of such a pair belongs
to $\mathbf U$.

Suppose instead that $\epsilon_j=1$, so that $d_j\in\mathbf U$.
In the first mixed product in \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-second-expanded}, set
$t=d_j$ and restrict $k$ to $\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}$.
In the last product in \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-second-expanded}, set $t=d_j$
and retain all $b\in\mathbf L$ satisfying $b<d_j$.
These factors give
\begin{equation}
E_j^{(1)}:=
\prod_{\substack{k\in\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}\\k<d_j}}
\frac{1-q^{-1}w_k^{-1}x_j}{1-w_k^{-1}x_j}
\prod_{\substack{b\in\mathbf L\\b<d_j}}
\frac{1-q^{-1}x_j^{-1}w_b}{1-x_j^{-1}w_b}.
\label{eq:allcard-E1-def}
\end{equation}
The remaining choices $k\in\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U$ in the first
mixed product (recall that $\mathbf K(\mathbf U)^{\mathrm c}
=\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}\sqcup(\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U)$) likewise
involve two indices in $\mathcal D$.
There is no factor involving $d_j$ and an index in $\mathbf L$ or
$\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}$ in \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-first-expanded}, because
every two-index factor in \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-first-expanded} contains
an index in $\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U$.

We next collect the factors involving two indices in $\mathcal D$,
including the choices left aside above.
Fix $d_i<d_j$, and write $x=x_i$, $y=x_j$.

If $(\epsilon_i,\epsilon_j)=(0,0)$, both indices belong to
$\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U$. In the product
\[
\prod_{\substack{a<b\\a,b\in\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U}}
\frac{1-qw_a^{-1}w_b^{-1}}{1-w_a^{-1}w_b^{-1}}
\]
in \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-first-expanded}, take $a=d_i$ and $b=d_j$.
The resulting factor is
\[
\frac{1-qw_{d_i}^{-1}w_{d_j}^{-1}}{1-w_{d_i}^{-1}w_{d_j}^{-1}}
=\frac{xy-q}{xy-1}=:\mathsf P_{00}(x,y).
\]
No mixed product in \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-first-expanded} contains this
pair, since both indices lie in $\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U$.
Nor does \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-second-expanded} contain a factor involving
this pair, since neither index belongs to $\mathbf U$.

If $(\epsilon_i,\epsilon_j)=(1,1)$, both indices belong to $\mathbf U$.
In the product
\[
\prod_{\substack{a<b\\a,b\in\mathbf U}}
\frac{1-q^{-1}w_a^{-1}w_b^{-1}}{1-w_a^{-1}w_b^{-1}}
\]
in \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-second-expanded}, take $a=d_i$ and $b=d_j$.
This gives
\[
\frac{1-q^{-1}w_{d_i}^{-1}w_{d_j}^{-1}}{1-w_{d_i}^{-1}w_{d_j}^{-1}}
=\frac{xy-q^{-1}}{xy-1}=:\mathsf P_{11}(x,y).
\]
No mixed product in \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-second-expanded} contains this
pair, and \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-first-expanded} contributes no factor
involving the pair because neither index belongs to
$\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U$.

If $(\epsilon_i,\epsilon_j)=(1,0)$, take $k=d_i\in\mathbf U$ and
$t=d_j\in\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U$ in the first mixed product in
\eqref{eq:allcard-SU-first-expanded}.
The condition $k<t$ holds, and the factor is
\[
\frac{1-qw_{d_i}^{-1}w_{d_j}}{1-w_{d_i}^{-1}w_{d_j}}
=\frac{x-qy}{x-y}=:\mathsf P_{10}(x,y).
\]
This is one of the choices $k\in\mathbf U$ left aside when defining
$E_j^{(0)}$. In the first mixed product in
\eqref{eq:allcard-SU-second-expanded}, the pair would require $k=d_j$
and $t=d_i$, which fails $k<t$.
The pair occurs in none of the other products: the two indices are in
different parts of $\mathcal D$, and neither belongs to $\mathbf L$ or
$\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}$.

Finally, if $(\epsilon_i,\epsilon_j)=(0,1)$, take
$k=d_i\in\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U$ and $t=d_j\in\mathbf U$ in the
first mixed product in \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-second-expanded}.
This gives
\[
\frac{1-q^{-1}w_{d_i}^{-1}w_{d_j}}{1-w_{d_i}^{-1}w_{d_j}}
=\frac{x-q^{-1}y}{x-y}=:\mathsf P_{01}(x,y).
\]
This is one of the choices $k\in\mathcal D\setminus\mathbf U$ left aside
when defining $E_j^{(1)}$.
In the first mixed product in \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-first-expanded},
the pair would require $k=d_j$ and $t=d_i$, again contrary to $k<t$.
As in the preceding case, none of the other products contains this pair.

Every two-index factor in \eqref{eq:allcard-SU-first-expanded} and
\eqref{eq:allcard-SU-second-expanded} has now been included either in
some $E_j^{(\epsilon_j)}$ or in some
$\mathsf P_{\epsilon_i\epsilon_j}(x_i,x_j)$.
By the definition of $S(\mathbf U)$ in
\eqref{eq:allcard-SU-def}, combining the factors computed above gives
\begin{align*}
S(\mathbf U)
={}&q^{\sum_jd_j\epsilon_j}
(1-q)^{m-|\mathbf U|}(1-q^{-1})^{|\mathbf U|}
\prod_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{1-x_j^{-2}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}E_j^{(\epsilon_j)}
\prod_{i<j}\mathsf P_{\epsilon_i\epsilon_j}(x_i,x_j).
\end{align*}
For $\mathbf U=\emptyset$, all $\epsilon_j$ are zero, so
\[
S(\emptyset)=(1-q)^m
\prod_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{1-x_j^{-2}}
\prod_{j=1}^{m}E_j^{(0)}\prod_{i<j}\mathsf P_{00}(x_i,x_j).
\]
Dividing these expressions, we obtain
\begin{equation}
\frac{S(\mathbf U)}{S(\emptyset)}
=(-1)^{|\mathbf U|}q^{\sum_j(d_j-1)\epsilon_j}
\prod_{j=1}^{m}\frac{E_j^{(\epsilon_j)}}{E_j^{(0)}}
\prod_{i<j}\frac{\mathsf P_{\epsilon_i\epsilon_j}(x_i,x_j)}
{\mathsf P_{00}(x_i,x_j)}.
\label{eq:allcard-SU-factor-ratio}
\end{equation}

We simplify the two products in this quotient separately.
We compare $E_j^{(1)}$ in \eqref{eq:allcard-E1-def} with
$E_j^{(0)}$ in \eqref{eq:allcard-E0-def} in the same way as in the
one-element case above. The product over $\mathbf L$ in
\eqref{eq:allcard-E1-def} is compared with the product over $\mathbf L$
in \eqref{eq:allcard-E0-def}, and likewise for $\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}$.
Each corresponding pair of factors again gives a ratio of $q^{-1}$.
For an element $a\in\mathbf L$ with $a<d_j$, the corresponding factors satisfy
\[
\frac{1-q^{-1}x_j^{-1}w_a}{1-x_j^{-1}w_a}
=q^{-1}\frac{1-qw_a^{-1}x_j}{1-w_a^{-1}x_j}.
\]
For an element $a\in\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}$ with $a<d_j$, they satisfy
\[
\frac{1-q^{-1}w_a^{-1}x_j}{1-w_a^{-1}x_j}
=q^{-1}\frac{1-qx_j^{-1}w_a}{1-x_j^{-1}w_a}.
\]
As in the one-element case, we count the indices in $\mathbf L$ or
$\mathbf M^{\mathrm c}$ smaller than $d_j$.
Of the $d_j-1$ smaller indices, $j-1$ now belong to $\mathcal D$,
namely $d_1,\ldots,d_{j-1}$, leaving $d_j-j$ factors of $q^{-1}$.
Hence
\[
\frac{E_j^{(\epsilon_j)}}{E_j^{(0)}}=q^{-(d_j-j)\epsilon_j}.
\]

From these expressions and the definitions \eqref{eq:allcard-hweights}
of $H_{ab}$, it is easy to check that
\[
\frac{\mathsf P_{\epsilon_i\epsilon_j}(x_i,x_j)}{\mathsf P_{00}(x_i,x_j)}
=q^{-\epsilon_j}
\frac{H_{\epsilon_i,\epsilon_j}(x_i,x_j)}{H_{00}(x_i,x_j)}.
\]
For each fixed $j$, there are $j-1$ choices of $i<j$.
Multiplying over all pairs therefore gives
\[
\prod_{i<j}\frac{\mathsf P_{\epsilon_i\epsilon_j}(x_i,x_j)}{\mathsf P_{00}(x_i,x_j)}
=q^{-\sum_j(j-1)\epsilon_j}
\prod_{i<j}\frac{H_{\epsilon_i,\epsilon_j}(x_i,x_j)}{H_{00}(x_i,x_j)}.
\]
Substituting the two product ratios into
\eqref{eq:allcard-SU-factor-ratio}, the total exponent of $q$ is
\[
\sum_j(d_j-1)\epsilon_j-\sum_j(d_j-j)\epsilon_j
-\sum_j(j-1)\epsilon_j=0.
\]
We conclude that
\[
\frac{S(\mathbf U)}{S(\emptyset)}
=(-1)^{|\mathbf U|}
\prod_{i<j}\frac{H_{\epsilon_i,\epsilon_j}(x_i,x_j)}{H_{00}(x_i,x_j)}.
\]
The denominator is independent of $\mathbf U$, and the correspondence
between $\mathbf U\subseteq\mathcal D$ and $\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^m$ is a
bijection, with $|\mathbf U|=|\epsilon|$. Therefore,
\[
\sum_{\mathbf U\subseteq\mathcal D}S(\mathbf U)
=\frac{S(\emptyset)}{\prod_{i<j}H_{00}(x_i,x_j)}
\sum_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^m}(-1)^{|\epsilon|}
\prod_{i<j}H_{\epsilon_i,\epsilon_j}(x_i,x_j).
\]
The final sum vanishes by Lemma~\ref{lem:allcard-universal-cancellation}, since
$(-1)^{|\epsilon|}=(-1)^m(-1)^{m-|\epsilon|}$.
Thus the matrix product \eqref{eq:allcard-boundary-product} has entry
zero when $\mathbf L\subsetneq\mathbf M$.
If $\mathbf L\not\subseteq\mathbf M$, every summand vanishes by
triangularity, while the diagonal entries are $1$, as shown above.
This proves \eqref{eq:allcard-Bhat-inverse} in type $C$.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
From \eqref{eq:allcard-P-factorization},
\[
\mathsf A^X(\mathbf w,q)^{-1}
=
\widehat\Phi(\mathbf w,q)^{-1}
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^X(\mathbf w,q)^{-1}.
\]
The first equality below is the $(\mathbf I,\mathbf L)$ entry of the
matrix product. The second follows from Lemma~\ref{lem:allcard-Phihat-inverse}
and Proposition~\ref{prop:allcard-Bhat-inverse}; the last equality uses
\eqref{eq:allcard-P-factorization}:
\begin{align}
\bigl(\mathsf A^X(\mathbf w,q)^{-1}\bigr)_{\mathbf I,\mathbf L}
&=
\sum_{\mathbf K\subset[N]}
\bigl(\widehat\Phi(\mathbf w,q)^{-1}\bigr)_{\mathbf I,\mathbf K}
\bigl(\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^X(\mathbf w,q)^{-1}\bigr)_{\mathbf K,\mathbf L}
\notag\\
&=
q^{|\lambda(\mathbf I)|-|\lambda(\mathbf L)|}
\sum_{\mathbf K\subset[N]}
\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^X_{\mathbf L^{\mathrm c},\mathbf K^{\mathrm c}}
(\mathbf w,q^{-1})
\widehat\Phi_{\mathbf K^{\mathrm c},\mathbf I^{\mathrm c}}
(\mathbf w,q^{-1})
\notag\\
&=
q^{|\lambda(\mathbf I)|-|\lambda(\mathbf L)|}
\left(\widehat{\mathrm{Br}}{}^X(\mathbf w,q^{-1})
\widehat\Phi(\mathbf w,q^{-1})\right)_{\mathbf L^{\mathrm c},\mathbf I^{\mathrm c}}
\notag\\
&=
q^{|\lambda(\mathbf I)|-|\lambda(\mathbf L)|}
\mathsf A^X_{\mathbf L^{\mathrm c},\mathbf I^{\mathrm c}}
(\mathbf w,q^{-1}).
\end{align}
The identity
$\mathsf A^X(\mathbf w,q)\mathsf A^X(\mathbf w,q)^{-1}=\mathrm{Id}$ is exactly
\eqref{eq:BC-all-cardinality-biorthogonality-matrix}; expanding the definition
\eqref{eq:allcard-A-def} gives
\eqref{eq:BC-all-cardinality-biorthogonality}.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- head-3: body_md -->
Take $N=1$ and $X=C$, and write $\mathbf w=(w_1)$.
By \eqref{eq:allcard-Delta-factor}, the diagonal values are
\begin{align*}
\Delta^C_{\emptyset}(\mathbf w,q)
&=\widetilde{WC}_{1,1,0}
(1\,|w_1\,|\emptyset)
=1-qw_1^2,\\
\Delta^C_{\{1\}}(\mathbf w,q)
&=\widetilde{WC}_{1,1,1}
(w_1\,|w_1\,|\{1\})
=1-w_1^{-2}.
\end{align*}
The remaining value needed below is
\[
\widetilde{WC}_{1,1,0}
(w_1\,|w_1\,|\emptyset)=1-q.
\]
<!-- end head-3 -->
<!-- head-4: body_md -->
Take $N=2$ and $X=C$.  By \eqref{eq:allcard-Delta-factor},
Theorem~\ref{RTVtheorem} $\mathrm{(ii)}$, and
\eqref{general-diagonal-factor},
\begin{align}
\Delta^C_{\{1\}}(\mathbf w,q)
&=(1-w_1^{-2})(1-qw_2^2)
\left(1-q\frac{w_2}{w_1}\right),
 \notag\\
\Delta^C_{\{2\}}(\mathbf w,q)
&=(1-w_2^{-2})(1-qw_1^2)
\left(1-\frac{w_1}{w_2}\right),
 \notag\\
\Delta^C_{\{2\}}(\mathbf w,q^{-1})
&=(1-w_2^{-2})(1-q^{-1}w_1^2)
\left(1-\frac{w_1}{w_2}\right).
 \notag
\end{align}
In terms of the normalized type $C$ functions, these are
\begin{align*}
\Delta^C_{\{1\}}(\mathbf w,q)
&=\widetilde{WC}_{2,2,1}
\bigl(w_1,1\,|\mathbf w\,|\{1\}\bigr),\\
\Delta^C_{\{2\}}(\mathbf w,q)
&=\widetilde{WC}_{2,2,1}
\bigl(w_2,1\,|\mathbf w\,|\{2\}\bigr),\\
\Delta^C_{\{2\}}(\mathbf w,q^{-1})
&=\left.\widetilde{WC}_{2,2,1}
\bigl(w_2,1\,|\mathbf w\,|\{2\}\bigr)
\right|_{q\mapsto q^{-1}}.
\end{align*}
The remaining values needed below are
\begin{align}
\widetilde{WC}_{2,2,1}
\bigl(w_2,1\,|\mathbf w\,|\{1\}\bigr)
&=(1-w_2^{-2})(1-qw_1^2)(1-q),
 \notag\\
\left.\widetilde{WC}_{2,2,1}
\bigl(w_2,1\,|\mathbf w\,|\{1\}\bigr)
\right|_{q\mapsto q^{-1}}
&=(1-w_2^{-2})(1-q^{-1}w_1^2)(1-q^{-1}).
 \notag
\end{align}
The support and lower-cardinality vanishing relations give
\begin{align}
\widetilde{WC}_{2,2,1}
\bigl(w_1,1\,|\mathbf w\,|\{2\}\bigr)&=0,
\label{eq:allcard-N2-WC12zero}\\
\widetilde{WC}_{2,2,2}
\bigl(w_i,1\,|\mathbf w\,|\{1,2\}\bigr)&=0
\qquad(i=1,2).
\label{eq:allcard-N2-WC2zero}
\end{align}
<!-- end head-4 -->
<!-- corollary-8: body_md -->
\label{cor:BC-branching-formula-inversion}
Let $X\in\{B,C\}$ and $0\le r\le\min(n,N)$.
Let $\mathbf K\subset[N]$ be an $r$-element subset, and let
$\mathbf M\subset[N]$ have cardinality
$0\le s=|\mathbf M|\le\min(n,N)$.
Then
\begin{align}
&\sum_{\substack{\mathbf I\subset[N]\\|\mathbf I|=r}}
\widetilde{WX}_{N,n,r}
(\mathbf w_{\mathbf M}\,|\mathbf w\,|\mathbf I)
\bigl(\Phi^{(r)}(\mathbf w,q)^{-1}\bigr)_{\mathbf I,\mathbf K}
=
\begin{cases}
\operatorname{Br}^X_{\mathbf M,\mathbf K}(\mathbf w,q),
&s\ge r\text{ and }\mathbf K\subset\mathbf M,\\[2mm]
0,&\text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:allcard-branching-inversion}
\end{align}
<!-- end corollary-8 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
If $s<r$, the normalized type $C$ and type $B$ functions vanish by
\eqref{eq:typeC-lower-cardinality-vanishing} and
\eqref{eq:typeB-lower-cardinality-vanishing}.  If $s\ge r$, substitute
\eqref{eq:allcard-branching-factorization}, interchange the two
finite sums, and use
$\Phi^{(r)}(\mathbf w,q)\Phi^{(r)}(\mathbf w,q)^{-1}=\mathrm{Id}$.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- thm-11: body_md -->
\cite[Theorems 3.1 and 6.7, Grassmannian specialization]{RTV}
\label{RTVtheorem}
Let
\[
T=(\mathbb C^\times)^N\times\mathbb C^\times
\]
act on $T^*\operatorname{Gr}(r,N)$, where the last factor scales the cotangent
fibers with character $h$.
Let
\[
\Gamma_1=\{\gamma_{1,1},\dots,\gamma_{1,r}\},
\qquad
\Gamma_2=\{\gamma_{2,1},\dots,\gamma_{2,N-r}\}.
\]
The equivariant $K$-theory ring is
\begin{align}
K_T\bigl(T^*\operatorname{Gr}(r,N)\bigr)
\simeq
\frac{
\mathbb C[\Gamma_1^{\pm1},\Gamma_2^{\pm1}]^{S_r\times S_{N-r}}
\otimes
\mathbb C[z_1^{\pm1},\dots,z_N^{\pm1},h^{\pm1}]
}{
\left\langle
f(\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)-f(z_1,\dots,z_N)
\ \middle|
f\in
\mathbb C[z_1^{\pm1},\dots,z_N^{\pm1}]^{S_N}
\right\rangle
}.
\label{eq:KT-Grassmannian}
\end{align}
Here $\Gamma_1$ and $\Gamma_2$ are the Chern roots of the tautological
subbundle and the tautological quotient bundle, respectively.
For $\sigma\in S_N$ and
$\mathcal I=(\mathbf{I},\mathbf{I}^{\mathrm{c}})$,
there exists a unique class
$
\kappa_{\sigma,\mathcal I}
\in
K_T\bigl(T^*\operatorname{Gr}(r,N)\bigr)
$
satisfying the following properties.

$\mathrm{(i)}$
For every fixed point
$
\mathcal M=(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm{c}}),
$
the restriction
$
\kappa_{\sigma,\mathcal I}\big|_{\mathcal M}
$
is divisible by $e_{\sigma,\mathcal M,-}^{\mathrm{vert}}$.

$\mathrm{(ii)}$
At the diagonal fixed point,
\[
\kappa_{\sigma,\mathcal I}\big|_{\mathcal I} =
P_{\sigma,\mathcal I}e_{\sigma,\mathcal I}.
\]

$\mathrm{(iii)}$
If
$
\mathcal M\ne\mathcal I,
$
then
$
\kappa_{\sigma,\mathcal I}\big|_{\mathcal M}
$
is $\mathcal M$-small, that is,
\[
\varphi_{\mathcal M}
\left(
\mathcal{N}\left(
\kappa_{\sigma,\mathcal I}\big|_{\mathcal M}
\right)
\right)
\subset
[0,r(N-r)-1].
\]
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
\kappa_{\sigma,\mathcal I}\big|_{\mathcal M} =
\widetilde W_{\sigma,\mathcal I}^{\mathrm{RTV}}
(\boldsymbol z_{\mathbf{M}},\boldsymbol z,h).
\label{kappa-Wtilde}
\end{equation}
<!-- end thm-11 -->
<!-- thm-12: body_md -->
\cite[Theorem 6.6, Grassmannian specialization]{RTV}
\label{thm:RTV-Gr-orthogonality}
For any two $r$-element subsets
$\mathbf J,\mathbf K\subset\{1,\ldots,N\}$, with
$\mathcal J=(\mathbf J,\mathbf J^{\mathrm c})$ and
$\mathcal K=(\mathbf K,\mathbf K^{\mathrm c})$, one has
\begin{align}
&\sum_{\substack{\mathbf M\subset\{1,\ldots,N\}\\|\mathbf M|=r}}
\frac{
h^{p(\mathbf K)}P_{\mathbf M}(\mathbf z)
}{
R_{\mathbf M}(\mathbf z)
Q_{\mathbf M}(\mathbf z,h)
}
\widetilde W_{\mathrm{id},\mathcal J}^{\mathrm{RTV}}
(\mathbf z_{\mathbf M},\mathbf z,h)
\widetilde W_{\sigma_0,\mathcal K}^{\mathrm{RTV}}
(\mathbf z_{\mathbf M}^{-1},\mathbf z^{-1},h^{-1})
=\delta_{\mathbf J,\mathbf K}.
\label{eq:RTV-Gr-orthogonality}
\end{align}
<!-- end thm-12 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
In the notation of \cite[Theorem~6.6]{RTV}, the ordered set partition has
only two blocks,
$\mathcal M=(\mathbf M,\mathbf M^{\mathrm c})$.
Thus the products $P(\mathbf z_{\mathcal M})$,
$R(\mathbf z_{\mathcal M})$, and $Q(\mathbf z_{\mathcal M},h)$ there reduce
exactly to
\eqref{eq:RTV-Gr-P}--\eqref{eq:RTV-Gr-Q}, and the inversion statistic reduces
to \eqref{eq:RTV-Gr-p}.  Substituting these expressions into
\cite[(6.7)]{RTV} gives \eqref{eq:RTV-Gr-orthogonality}.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
